## Pagina 4

**2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

# FOREWORDS

On behalf of the FIM, it gives me great pleasure to welcome you all to another season of racing to decide the 2026 FIM Motocross World Championship titles, and with twenty rounds spread across seventeen countries and five continents to determine the best MXGP and MX2 riders on the planet,

we are guaranteed an action-packed year.

Of all the disciplines on the FIM calendar, Motocross has to be one of the most thrilling and spectacular. Incredibly intense and requiring skill, mental fortitude and supreme physical conditioning, the FIM Motocross World Championship represents the absolute pinnacle of the sport where only the very best can hope to compete.

For the fourth consecutive year, Argentina will have the honour of staging the opening round with the action returning to the east of the South American country at a new venue at Bariloche in early March before competitors head to Europe for the MXGP of Andalucia at Almonte in southern Spain, another circuit that is new to the series.

From here, the championship travels north to more familiar surroundings at Frauenfeld in Switzerland at the end of March, before back-to-back rounds in Italy in April, starting with the MXGP of Sardegna at Riola Sardo, followed by the MXGP of Trentino at Pietramurata.

While the venue for round six is still to be announced, we know round seven will be staged in late May at Lacapelle Marival in France followed by a visit to Germany’s iconic Teutschenthal at the end of month before rounds in Kegums in Latvia, Montevarchi in Italy and Águeda in Portugal are staged in June.

History will be made at the beginning of July when the FIM Motocross World Championship returns to South Africa for the first time in eighteen years – renewing a long-standing connection that was first forged as far back as 1985 – before another significant moment when the series revisits Foxhills, scene of a series of epic British GPs in the 1990s, for the first time in over a quarter of a century.

The fast and spectacular hardpack of Loket in the Czech Republic is the venue for round fourteen at the end of July, before, by way of complete contrast, the deep and unforgiving sand of Lommel in Belgium gets August’s action underway.

The same month will also see rounds at Uddevalla in Sweden and Arnhem in the Netherlands before the racing leaves Europe for September’s three concluding rounds at Afyonkarahisar in Turkiye, Shanghai in China and, for the second consecutive season, Darwin in Australia.

Another successful season of top-flight racing will conclude with the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations, which will be staged at the hugely popular Ernée circuit in France over the first weekend of October.

This year’s FIM Women’s Motocross World Championship will again be decided over six rounds with the series getting under way in late April at a venue to be announced before rounds at Lacapelle Marival, Teutschenthal, Foxhills and Arnhem are followed by the deciding round at Darwin.

We wish our series promoter Infront Moto Racing, all competitors, local organisers, national federations, FIM officials, teams, sponsors, media and, of course, the passionate fans who are the lifeblood of the sport, a safe and prosperous season of exciting racing.

**Jorge Viegas** FIM President

**4**

## Pagina 5

**2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

# FOREWORDS

On behalf of the FIM/ CMS allow me to welcome all of our FIM MXGP & MX2 supporters to San Carlos de Bariloche where + _Eventos_ , led by David Eli, have brought the best Motocross Championship in the world to one of the most beautiful places in the planet: The Argentinian Patagonia.

Despite not going to our most beloved La Angostura, this time Infront Motor Racing, our partners and promoters, jointly with + _Eventos_ have prepared a brand-new venue by the Nahuel Haupi lake where the landscape with snow capped peaks is breathtaking, but also the warmth of its dwellers will make you feel like at home.

Argentina has been stablished as the season opener due to many factors, though the end the Austral summer is one of them, + _Eventos_ , that have been awarded best MXGP & MX2 Organizer in the past, have largely proved to be able to rapidly adapt and prepare an MXGP & MX2 venue at the highest standard required in modern FIM Championships.

The start of the season will see some riders embarked on new adventures to proof and test their skills with different brands. Needless to say, that their ambition remains the same and after a year-long season, they are eager to hold on to the FIM Motocross World Championship title which not only does it bear genuine prestige, but it also offers the motocross riders an exclusive place in the FIM _Olympus_ Museum.

What can be taken for granted is all the expectations that have been lurking during a very long and cold winter. There is still a long journey ahead, but the motocross riders, teams, and families are committed to enduring it together. Brighter days await and all the MXGP & MX2 followers can’t wait to see how the 2026 season unveils.

Once more, I would like to show our gratitude to those who have worked behind the spotlight and have made everything look easy and ready for action.

**Antonio Alía Portela** FIM/CMS Director

**5**

## Pagina 6

**2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

# FOREWORDS

## Dear MXGP Friends,

We are proud and excited to once again begin the FIM Motocross World Championship season in Argentina, a country that has become a true pillar of MXGP over the years. While the opening round now moves to the spectacular new location of Bariloche, starting

the season in Argentina has become a real MXGP tradition, and one that perfectly reflects the passion and energy of this nation.

The 2026 rider line-up is simply one of the most exciting motocross has ever seen. This season opens with an unprecedented concentration of talent, experience and ambition, highlighted by two major factory moves that have reshaped the landscape of MXGP: Jeffrey Herlings joining Honda and Tim Gajser moving to Yamaha. These landmark changes alone set the stage for an explosive championship battle from the very first gate drop.

Alongside them, several established World Champions arrive fully motivated to further expand their already impressive record including reigning Champion Romain Febvre and Maxime Renaux. At the same time, a new generation of outstanding talent has stepped up to the premier class, already proving they are more than ready to challenge the established order. Lucas Coenen, runner-up in his rookie MXGP season behind Febvre, has already emerged as a serious threat, while MX2 World Champions Kay de Wolf and Andrea Adamo enter the top class eager to make an immediate impact. Adding even more depth to this extraordinary field is the return of two-time MX2 World Champion Tom Vialle, further strengthening an already exceptional grid. Quite remarkably, the MXGP starting gate alone will feature eight World Champions, a level of quality that underlines just how extraordinary the 2026 season is set to be.

In MX2 Simon Längenfelder will come back after winning his first World Title in 2025, and try to retain his crown which is one of the hardest thing to do in MX2 with many contenders getting more experienced and threatening with the likes of Liam Everts and Sacha Coenen.

Set against the breathtaking Patagonian landscape, Bariloche will provide a stunning and unique backdrop for the first round. This new venue will host the opening chapter of what promises to be one of the strongest and most competitive MXGP line-ups seen in many years, with an exceptional depth of talent across both MXGP and MX2.

Launching the season at a brand-new circuit always brings a special challenge. With no previous references, this will ensure some unpredictability, intensity and pure racing from the very first gate drop. It is the perfect way to ignite a new championship battle.

As MXGP continues to grow globally, our digital ecosystem has also continued its strong upward trajectory in 2025. Across Instagram, Facebook, TikTok, YouTube, and X, MXGP added more than 780,000 new followers and subscribers, driven by video-first storytelling and authentic race-weekend content. Instagram closed the year with 1.42 million followers, generating 293 million impressions (+92%), while Facebook reached 3.23 million fans and recorded 55 million video views (+253%). TikTok confirmed itself as our fastest-growing platform, climbing to 459k followers with 92.9 million video views. YouTube delivered 34.45 million views and an exceptional 413k new subscribers, while X continued to serve as a key real-time information platform with over 100k followers. Behind-the-scenes content and innovative formats have never brought fans closer to the sport.

I would like to sincerely thank the organiser, David Eli, local authorities and the Argentinian Motorcycle Federation for their continued commitment and enthusiasm in bringing MXGP back to Argentina. Their dedication plays a vital role in the global success of our championship. I would also like to thank the FIM, FIM Latina, our partners, manufacturers, teams, riders, and media for their constant support and pledge to the success of MXGP.

On behalf of Infront Moto Racing, I wish everyone an unforgettable opening round in Bariloche and an incredible start to the 2026 MXGP season.

**David Luongo** President and CEO Infront Moto Racing

# ROMAIN FEBVRE 2025 MXGP WORLD CHAMPION BACK TO THE TOP! ROMAIN FEBVRE WINS HIS SECOND TITLE A DECADE ON FROM HIS FIRST!

**AS THE 2025 SEASON DAWNED, IT WAS CLEAR THAT LONG-STANDING CONTENDERS SUCH AS ROMAIN FEBVRE AND TIM GAJSER WOULD ONCE MORE BE TOWARDS THE FRONT, BUT MXGP WAS FACING A SEASON WITHOUT ITS REIGNING CHAMPION, JORGE PRADO, AND ONE IF ITS OTHER MAJOR STARS, JEFFREY HERLINGS, YET AGAIN RULED OUT OF THE EARLY RACES WITH A WINTER KNEE INJURY. HOWEVER, ONE WELCOME ADDITION CAME IN THE FORM OF PRADO’S REPLACEMENT AT THE DE CARLI SQUAD, NOW RETURNING TO THE KTM BRAND, AS EXCITING TEENAGER LUCAS COENEN MOVED UP TO THE MXGP CATEGORY AFTER WINNING MORE GPS THAN ANYONE IN THE 2024 MX2 SERIES.**

## Pagina 53

2025 CHAMPIONS **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

Febvre had been strong in the 2024 campaign when present, but a thumb injury at his home GP derailed his title challenge just when he was catching fire, and he was unable to compete for the whole year, eventually claiming a single race win in China on his way to fifth in the series. Of course, the veteran Frenchman has been considered a contender for every MXGP title since he surprised many by claiming the crown in his very first year in the class. Back in 2015, much of the pre-season hype had been focussed on the upcoming battle between multiple Champion Antonio Cairoli and the incoming American legend Ryan Villopoto. Few eyes were on the French rookie with the MX2 bronze medal around his neck and just a single GP win to his name.

In what was a particularly tough year, however, injuries to both the pre-season favourites, as well as to further contenders such as Max Nagl and Clement Desalle, brought the Yamaha man into focus, breaking through with a famous first MXGP victory on home soil at Villars-sous-Ecot, the first of 8 GP wins in the final 11 rounds of the series. He took the title a round early, in Mexico, and completed his joy with a win at the final round in California plus a dominating performance as part of a victorious Team France at the Motocross of Nations at Ernée!

Since then, injuries have kept him away from the crown when he often looked to be in contention. Engaged in a stirring battle with Tim Gajser, as well as Cairoli, in 2016, a big crash at Matterley Basin put paid to his chances of defending the crown. Never quite the same force on Yamaha during the 2017 & ’18 seasons while KTM dominated with Cairoli and new arrival Jeffrey Herlings, an early-season injury held him back in 2019 before he came back to take his final GP win in blue at Loket that season.

## Pagina 55

2025 CHAMPIONS **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

His switch to Kawasaki in 2020 earned him a single GP victory and two race wins, before being part of the stellar three-way tussle with Gajser and Herlings for the 2021 crown. Wearing the red plate into the last two rounds of the season, Febvre was only just unable to resist the force of nature that was Jeffrey Herlings in the final round at Mantova, a heartbreaker that he still tries not to think about.

Following that disappointment, a broken leg sustained in the winter’s Paris Supercross effectively ended the following year’s campaign before it began. As the factory Kawasaki squad changed management to Kimi Raikkonen’s Ice One rig, with Antti Pyrhönen as Team Manager, Febvre had to wait until the second half of the year to race again, and amazingly won a race at the very end of the season to climb the podium in Türkiye.

That result proved that Romain was far from done, and although 2023 started inconsistently, he was clearly a challenger from the off, although a small concussion in the Qualifying Race in Spain left him with zero points for the weekend. He bounced back to win his first race of the year in his home GP two weeks later, and proceeded to win six out of seven GPs in an effort to haul in the points lead of Jorge Prado. Ultimately, a mechanical failure in the first race of the penultimate round at Maggiora finally buried his chances of the title, and he was second in the series once more.

of it. All season long, it felt like the young Belgian was closing in, but with an amazing 14-round streak of podium results, plus stunning victories in Spain, France, Great Britain, and Sweden, the Kawasaki man was making it more and more difficult.

His consistency through the final three rounds was perfect, holding off a late charge from the teenager in Türkiye, then taking precious points in China while Coenen wilted in the heat, left him with an easy job in Australia, at a track which he instantly enjoyed.

The man born on New Year’s Eve 1991 finally joined the ranks of two-time World Champions, with the most incredible ten-year gap between titles, double the previous record of time between consecutive gold medals for any rider, and joined Frederic Bolley, Mickael Pichon, Jean-Michel Bayle, Sebastien Tortelli, Marvin Musquin, and Tom Vialle on the list of double World Champions from France! His 26 career GP wins put him just inside the top 20 all-time, and you even get the feeling that he is far from finished as he turns 34 at the turn of the year! Will the big #3 be swapped for the #1? We certainly hope so, but there is no doubting that Romain Febvre’s second world title was just reward for a decade of undying effort, and richly deserved for one of the modern game’s most underestimated MXGP warriors. Felicitations, Monsieur Febvre!

After his 2024 disappointment, Febvre entered 2025 with lots of talk about the “ten-year gap”. With Tim Gajser exiting the Championship through injury in Switzerland, the same day as teenage tearaway Lucas Coenen took his first MXGP class GP victory, it was the Frenchman who was the obvious candidate to take over the red plate and see what he could make

## Pagina 58

2025 CHAMPIONS **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

# SIMON LÄNGENFELDER 2025 MX2 WORLD CHAMPION

## **2025 MX2 SEASON**

Sometimes the quiet guys are the strongest in the room. Amongst a pack of whip-throwing, wheeliehappy showmen, the smooth, conservative, consistent Simon Längenfelder would not often stand out in a practice session, until you check the lap times. As would usually be the case for a rider born in eastern Germany, just half an hour from the Czech border, Simon is strongest on a slick, hardpack surface that rewards his meticulous precision. A cheerful character by nature, he is a deadly serious racer who has deservedly risen to the status of MX2 World Champion at the end of his sixth Grand Prix campaign.

Längenfelder had burst into prominence from relative obscurity with a stunning perfect day at Matterley Basin, the opening round of the 2022 MX2 World Championship, to record the first victory for the GASGAS brand in the class. Still two months from his 18<sup>th</sup> birthday, he had finished tenth in the 2021 series, his first full GP campaign after an interrupted debut season in 2020. His best in 2021 was sixth overall, at the British venue as well, so the 2022 opener was not only his first win but also his first career podium. Suddenly German Motocross fans had a new hero to cheer for.

|**RIDER PROFILE**||
|---|---|
|NAME|SIMON LÄNGENFELDER|
|DATE OF BIRTH|27<sup>TH</sup>APRIL 2004|
|NATIONALITY|GERMAN|
|TEAM
RED BU|LL KTM FACTORY RACING|

Unable to repeat that podium success until three months later in Spain, it was clear that the youngster was not quite ready to be a title challenger. Three further podium results on the hardpack of Indonesia, Czechia, and Sweden put him a solid third in the Championship however, and the ever-smiling German had established himself as a contender to talk about for future seasons.

**58**

## Pagina 59

2025 CHAMPIONS **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

Although 2023 started a little slower in comparison, a race win at Trentino and 1-1 domination of round six in Spain put him towards the sharp end of the points, but a sudden training injury put him out of three GPs and that was that. He did recover to take his third career GP win, this time in Sweden, but a worrying trend emerged in the last four GPs of the year. He won his first GP race in sand at Arnhem, the first of the day, but was unable to bring home the GP win at the end of the day. This story repeated itself in Türkiye, Italy, and even at Matterley Basin for the final round. While lifting himself above Liam Everts to claim the bronze medal again, it must have been a frustrating end to the year.

That frustration continued into 2024. For the fifth GP in a row, he won race one but could not convert it to a GP win at the opener in Argentina. With the Nestaan Husqvarna teammates of Kay de Wolf and Lucas Coenen battling for the title from the outset, Längenfelder was actually unable to clinch a single GP victory all season. Only at Trentino, Lommel – proving again that he could deliver on sand – and in Switzerland did he see the chequered flag first again. His consistency, scoring top ten in every race apart from a crash that broke his collarbone in the mud of Portugal, kept him third in the series again, but there was still a question mark over the 20-year-old’s ability to battle for a title, despite the amazing toughness he had shown by riding through the pain following that collarbone surgery.

The early season build-up for 2025 had seen a lot of focus on the Coenen brothers, who were joining the German under the De Carli awning as it returned to KTM colours. Lucas was not to be a rival anymore, however, as he was replacing Jorge Prado by taking on MXGP, but Simon still had to contend with reigning Champion De Wolf, who was still seething from a brutal incident between the two in Türkiye. Andrea Adamo looked stronger again, and Liam Everts was

surprisingly fast in his return from his Chinese back injury. Sacha Coenen, as always, was fast off the start and had begun to stay on more, as evidenced by his second race victory in Argentina.

For his own part, Simon had featured in the first episode of Behind The Gate, and it was very clear that he was determined to shake off this tag of “Mr. Third Place”. Gone was the slightly goofy “pleased to be here” grin of previous years, and his Bavarian jaw looked set towards turning bronze into gold. Only compatriot Larissa Papenmeier and 1980s legend Kees van der Ven had also taken bronze medals in three consecutive years, and for sure Längenfelder wanted to break their trends of never getting to the very top of a title chase. To this end, he had changed his race number from his old EMX tag of 516, into a very stylish #27, echoing the birth date for both himself and his lovely partner Malin, as well as the date of that first GP win in England.

**59**

## Pagina 60

2025 CHAMPIONS **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

It was all looking good in the second Argentine race at Cordoba as he closed in on the overall win with a 2-2, until the heat appeared to overwhelm him! He slumped to the ground twice in the final laps and finished an exhausted sixth, dropping to third overall. The following few rounds were underwhelming, with a race win at St Jean d’Angely and a Quali win in Sardegna the only highlights as De Wolf seemed to be easing away from everybody.

That all changed with a smoking 1-1 display at Frauenfeld, Simon’s second GP win at the circuit and one where he only saw lapped riders in front of him for the whole day, taking both Fox Holeshot Awards and closing to within three points of the reigning Champ. This was the Längenfelder that his team had been hoping for, and although Adamo was also close, the German had put himself forward as the main challenger to De Wolf.

A bar-banging battle between the two followed in the mud of Portugal, where they scored equal points, but it was in France where things started to unravel for the Husqvarna man. A crash in the Qualifying Race had left the Dutchman with a shoulder injury, and suddenly the German was wearing the red plate for the first time since that 2022 British win, right into his home GP at Teutschenthal!

Simon has never been his best at his home GP, although the Czech round at Loket is geographically closer to his home town of Hof, and he lost the red plate to Adamo after a muddy weekend, but a steady ride in Latvia took it back! Again there were question marks, as a last lap crash cost the German the GP win, but a perfect weekend at his old favourite Matterley followed to leave him with a suddenly massive gap of 52 points over Adamo, with De Wolf another 18 back! Now the real mental test would come – could Simon hold back a charging Kay, especially with the big sand races of Finland, Flanders, and the Netherlands still to come?

While the 2024 Champ predictably won all six races in the soft stuff, with the KTM man averaging just sixth place through them all, Längenfelder brilliantly countered each sand GP with overall victories in the Czech Republic, Sweden, and Türkiye. Even so, with just the heat of China and Australia to come, and the memory of his issues in Argentina still fresh, the 12-point gap between the leading pair looked ominous for the German.

Crashes for De Wolf at Shanghai extended that gap to 16, as Adamo officially exited the title race, but a last lap pass by the Italian had cost Simon three points, and there was a worry as to how important those points would be!

**60**

## Pagina 61

2025 CHAMPIONS **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

The two contenders were never in the same airspace on Saturday in Australia as De Wolf closed in by a further three points, but starting together on Sunday’s opening race saw a foreshadow of what was to come, as the Dutchman dived inside his rival on the opening lap, with the German brilliantly countering the assault to stretch away, proceeding to lead for seven laps before the inevitable happened, in brutal fashion!

The pair collided, with the red plate holder coming off worse, but with such a distance over third place that he was lucky to be able to remount and take second. One race to go, and with a ten-point gap it was clear that De Wolf would need to attack early!

He did exactly that, and at the end of the first lap the pair made contact again, and once more Längenfelder was on the ground! With his teammate Coenen out front after another blazing Holeshot, Simon remounted as the rain hit in biblical fashion, suddenly transforming the lush red soil into an absolute quagmire. Stumbling in an unexpectedly deep corner, the German crashed again from sixth place, getting back up in 11<sup>th</sup> and looking in big trouble as Kay closed in on Sacha!

Suddenly, the #1 Husqvarna seemed to be slowing, as puddles were building everywhere! He wasn’t alone, as many others started to splutter to a halt, engines drowning in all directions. With Längenfelder recovering to sixth, De Wolf needed to win, but he couldn’t close on Coenen, and finally, on the very corner where he had put his rival on the floor, the Dutchman’s bike cried enough, forcing him to push it through, stuttering on for a few more turns before finally stopping completely! Two laps later the race was called off, and on countback De Wolf was credited with fifth place, remarkably giving him the GP victory, but the title was Längenfelder’s after a brave recovery from his early issues!

It was a brilliant season-long performance for the first German World Champion since Ken Roczen in 2011, and a genuine victory for the quiet man who has always gone about his work in a no-nonsense, methodical fashion. In a Motocross world where the sand demons tend to be triumphant, here was a big win for the hardpack specialist, for a rider who is silky smooth with sublime throttle control when the going gets slick. The future will hold many challenges for the likeable German in the years to come, but he will always be able to enjoy the memory of a season where he silenced the critics to become a regular GP winner, and the 2025 World Champion in MX2! Prost, Herr Längenfelder!

# ROUND 04 MXGP OF SARDEGNA (ITA) RIOLA SARDO

## **EMX125 ROUND 03 | EMX250 ROUND 03 11-12 APRIL 2026**

|**AIRPORTS**||
|---|---|
|**Name**|**Aeroporto di Cagliari/Elmas**|
|**Distance**|105.8 km|
|**Website**|)**www.sogaer.it/it**|
|**Name**|**Aeroporto di Alghero/Fertilia**|
|**Distance**|150 km|
|**Website**|)**www.aeroportodialghero.it**|
|**Name**|**Aeroporto di Olbia Costa Smeralda**|
|**Distance**|180 km|
|**Website**|)**www.geasar.it**|

### **ROAD DIRECTIONS**

- **From Cagliari**

Distanza Km 116, percorrere la S.S. 131 in direzione Cagliari/Sassari. Uscire dalla S.S. 131 ad “Oristano Nord” e proseguire sulla S.P. 12 attraversando gli abitati di Zeddiani - Baratili San Pietro - Riola Sardo. Giunti a Riola Sardo, la S.P. 12 si immetterà sulla S.S. 292 e proseguire con direzione di marcia verso l’abitato di Cuglieri (OR) / Spiagge del Sinis. Da Riola Sardo e proseguendo sulla S.S. 292 seguire le indicazioni (dopo la 1^ rotonda svoltare al secondo incrocio a sinistra su strada vicinale)

- **From Sassari**

Distanza Km 150, percorrere la S.S. 131 in direzione Sassari/Cagliari. Da qui, seguire le indicazioni del punto A;

- **From Olbia-Golfo di Aranci**

- Distanza Km 180, può decidere se percorrere la S.S. 131 D.C.N. raggiungendo Nuoro e proseguendo sulla medesima fino ad Abbasanta (OR) per poi proseguire sulla S.S. 131 in direzione Cagliari fino all’uscita Oristano Nord, oppure, raggiungere e percorrere la E840 Berchidda - Oschiri - Chilivani - Bonnannaro - Torralba e da qui immettersi sulla S.S. 131 in direzione Cagliari fino all’uscita Oristano Nord.

- **From Arbatax**

- Distanza 169 km, percorrere la S.S. 389 VAR in direzione Nuoro, immettersi sulla S.S.131 D.C.N. in direzione Abbasanta (OR), immettersi sulla S.S.131 in direzione Cagliari ed uscire su Oristano Nord.

The Motocross Track “Le Dune “is 15 km from Oristano.

## Pagina 136

MXGP HISTORY **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

# HISTORY OF THE FIM MOTOCROSS WORLD CHAMPIONSHIP

**The history of Motocross began in the early 20**<sup>**th**</sup> **century when the first events took place in Great Britain, specifically during the 1920s when the first competitions known as “scrambles” took place. Once the World War II ended, the sport started to grow from a national to an international level with the very first team event known as Motocross des Nations, racing with 500cc machines, took place in 1947 in the Netherlands. Although the Belgian rider Auguste Mingels was faster, the team classification prevailed, and Great Britain claimed the first Trophy. From then on, this special event was held regularly every year, and was mostly dominated by the British team until the end of the 1960s.**

**The Trophée des Nations, team event for 250cc machines, was added in 1961, and the Coupe des Nations (for teams with 125cc motorcycles) in 1981. In 1984, the rules were changed: the three classes would race together, each time entering three riders. Currently America is the most successful Nation in this event which they have won 22 times. Back in the 50s, on the individual level, Motocross started to grow fast, with many events held in various countries. This led the FIM to introduce the first European Championship in 1952. That maiden series was open to 500cc motorcycles and the first winner was Belgian Victor Leloup onboard an FN machine. He was followed by August Mingels in ‘53 and ‘54, before British riders John Draper and Leslie Archer took over. But another country was also reaching the top in Motocross – individual and team – in those days, beside Great Britain and Belgium, that country was Sweden.**

## **1957-1972**

## **1973-1990**

**From European to World Championship. Joel Robert (250cc) and Roger De Coster (500cc) make their name.**

After five years of European Championship, Motocross took an important step forward with the series being promoted to the World Championship status in 1957 – winner was Swedish Bill Nilsson onboard an AJS machine. Still in 1957 the first European Cup for 250cc motorcycles was created to match the popularity of this upcoming discipline. This Cup would last only two years to be promoted to the European Championship status in 1959. Three years later in 1962, the 250cc class had its first World Championship with the eventual winner being legendary Husqvarna mounted Torsten Hallman from Sweden. The Swedish won the Title for two consecutive years until upcoming talent Joël Robert from Belgium steered his CZ to victory collecting the first of six titles – a record which would remain unbeaten until 2002. Robert would win another five 250cc World Titles from 1968 until 1972, the year of birth of his countryman Stefan Everts, the man who will break all the records 30 years later.

As Japanese manufacturers entered the series in the late 60s, Joël Robert was the first World Championship winner onboard such a machine as he steered the Suzuki to victory in the 250cc class in 1970. One year later, Suzuki would win another 250cc World Title with Robert but success came also in the 500cc class. Now a myth in the sport, in 1971, Roger De Coster and Suzuki won their first 500cc Championship which was also the first of five World Titles for De Coster – all in the 500cc class.

### **The arrival of the 125cc World Championship. Eric Geboers and Jean-Michel Bayle great ambassadors of Motocross.**

In 1973, another class, the 125cc, had its dedicated European Championship, which was won for two consecutive years by Belgian André Malherbe on a Zundapp. In 1975 it became a World Championship and Gaston Rahier won the Title straight away onboard a Suzuki, the first manufacturer to ever claim a Title in all three classes.

Malherbe would exploit his early 125cc experience to collect the 500cc World Title in 1980 and 1981 as Belgium was shaping up like the most talented country for Motocross. In 1975 another Belgian, Harry Everts, took his first 250cc World Title. He will win another three Championships in his career before retiring and raising his child Stefan.

In 1982, another star entered the World Motocross Championship with Eric Geboers winning the 125cc Championship after Everts had dominated it for three consecutive years – before Everts, Japanese Akira Watanabe took the 125cc crown in 1978. Geboers would storm through the field to remarkably win all three World Titles 125cc, 250cc and 500cc granting him the nickname of Mr. 875, the sum of 125, 250 and 500. Basically Eric Geboers was the first man to ever win one World Title in each category.

Georges Jobe and Dave Thorpe would also write their names in the record books of the World Champions in these years, while exceptional young guns would come up to establish the base of the next decades of World Motocross.

**136**

## Pagina 137

MXGP HISTORY **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

French Jean-Michel Bayle and Italian Alex Puzar won their first titles in 1988 and in 1990 respectively. As Puzar went on for more success in the series, Bayle first moved to Supercross, then to Road Racing collecting strong results and becoming a legend for his incredible talent in motorsports.

# **1991-2000**

**New generation four stroke machines enter and win World Titles. Stefan Everts begins his winning streak.**

This decade of World Motocross started with the Title of Stefan Everts, currently the most successful Motocross rider ever with 10 World Titles and 101 GP wins to his credit. Onboard a Suzuki, Everts – son of Harry Everts – became World Champion in the 125cc class in 1991 to be succeeded by Greg Albertijn in 1992 as the latter became the first South African to collect a World Title in Motocross.

Albertijn would win other two World Titles in the 250cc class as the 125cc class saw Italian Alessio “Chicco” Chiodi win the Championship for three consecutive years from 1997 until 1999.

Sebastien Tortelli, another of Everts’ main rivals throughout his career together with Albertijn, also passed by the 125cc World Championship, which he won in 1996, before winning in 250cc in 1998.

French countryman Frederic Bolley succeeded to Tortelli in 1999 and 2000 winning two consecutive 250cc Titles.

With new generation four stroke machines becoming popular in the GP campaign in 1999 came the first World Title. Yamaha mounted Andrea Bartolini was the first rider to win a Championship with such a machine beating Joel Smets, the 500cc Title winner in the previous two years and also in 2000.

# **2001-2006**

## **Youthstream celebrates Stefan Everts and the new generation during the years of records. Women and Veterans racing dedicated series.**

The last years have seen Motocross develop very quickly courtesy of the professional work of new promoter Youthstream headed by President Giuseppe Luongo, a man who had been involved in Motocross since 1983. Youthstream took the World Motocross Championships and the Motocross of Nations to the next level as the series increased the visits overseas and benefitted from a strong TV and media coverage. Nowadays the riders involved in the Championships come from more than 30 different nations, the races are watched onsite by more than half a million spectators, as the number of TV viewers overtakes one billion.

In 2004 the classes were renamed. The 125cc was called MX2 and is now hosting mainly 250cc four stroke bikes. The 250cc class was renamed MX1 hosting 450cc four stroke machines as the former 500cc class became an open class, the MX3.

The professional set up was matched by excellent racing and new talents going through the ranks such as Steve Ramon, Ben Townley, Antonio “Tony” Cairoli, Tyla Rattray, Tommy Searle just to name a few.

Though this decade will be remembered for the breakthrough of Stefan Everts. The Belgian went on to take his 10<sup>th</sup> World Title and his 101<sup>st</sup> GP victory in 2006, his last year of professional racing before retiring and becoming Team Manager for the factory KTM Motocross Team. Everts set the records, which are now the ultimate challenge of the whole younger generations of professional Motocross racers.

# **2007-2019**

**The youngsters leave their mark on the FIM Motocross World Championship. Philippaerts, Cairoli, Desalle, Musquin, Roczen, Herlings, Febvre, Gajser and many more set the Championship on fire.**

These years the young guns of the FIM Motocross World Championship have established their authority on the series. Ramon succeeded to Everts in the MX1 series in 2007 after the season’s dominator Coppins was taken out by a shoulder injury. Italian Cairoli took back the MX2 Title, as 2006 World Champion Pourcel was unable to defend the crown due to an up and down season which ended in a severe back injury.

After two years of team management, Stefan Everts celebrated the first World Title with Tyla Rattray winning the MX2 World Championship in 2008. That was also the year David Philippaerts won his maiden World Title in the MX1 despite it being only his second year in contention.

Year by year the level and speed that Grand Prix riders ride at progresses and now the most skilled ones are aiming to beat the older generations’ records. In 2010 Cairoli decided to ride the revolutionary KTM 350cc on which the Italian claimed his fourth, fifth, sixth, seventh and eighth World Titles in six consecutive years. The Italian is breaking all the records, and continues to do so as he has won seventy-two Grand Prix’s and sits second on the list of most Grand Prix wins, twenty nine behind ‘The King’ Stefan Everts.

In the MX2, young German talent Ken Roczen joined KTM and obtained his maiden World Title in 2011 in front of his home crowd in Gaildorf, Germany. The talented German also wrote his name in the motocross history of his country before moving to the USA to live his American dream. With Roczen gone, 2012 and 13 have been dominated by Dutch phenomenon Jeffrey Herlings. At the ripe age of eighteen, Herlings has already added two FIM MX2 World Titles to his list of career achievements, which he obtained with two near perfect seasons. 2014 saw Herlings line-up for his third consecutive MX2 World Title with his main goal being to win every Grand Prix.

**137**

## Pagina 138

MXGP HISTORY **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

In 2014, Red Bull KTM Factory Racing’s Antonio Cairoli continued to chip away at Stefan Everts ten FIM Motocross World Championship titles taking title number eight in Trindade, Brazil. Jeremy Van Horebeek also stamped his name on the championship as the most consistent rider in the MXGP class banking eleven consecutive podium finishes. After missing the podium at round 13 in Finland, the Belgian bounced back at round 14 in the Czech Republic to take home his first ever MXGP class win.

The twenty-four year old finished the season as the vice MXGP world champion. While the racing in 2014 was amazing, all attention had already turned to 2015 with the championship expected to blow all other seasons out of the water. In 2015 MXGP’s talent pool is deeper than ever especially with the arrival of American legend, multi-time AMA supercross and motocross champion, Ryan Villopoto. Villopoto is the first American with a résumé as rich as his to make the transition from the AMA series’ to the FIM Motocross World Championship.

2014 produced one of the best seasons of MX2 racing the championship is yet to witness, starting with young French star Dylan Ferrandis’ win of one race at round one in Qatar. The on-watchers were shocked, to say the least, that the usual dominating force Jeffrey Herlings appeared weaker than he had over the previous two seasons. The Dutch sensation Herlings then went on to admit he was recovering from a leg injury.

After not getting off to the greatest of starts, which allowed the likes of Arnaud Tonus to claim his first ever MX2 round win and Glenn Coldenhoff to take his first ever race win, things went from bad to worst for the two-time FIM MX2 World Champ Herlings when he broke his femur racing for charity right on the heels of wrapping up his third consecutive FIM MX2 World Championship. With Herlings out MX2 became 100% unpredictable, each race was intense from start to finish and each first time win for riders such as Jordi Tixier, Romain Febvre, Max Anstie and Tim Gajser was hard fought and earned.

In a heroic effort to clinch the title despite suffering from an unhealed broken femur, Herlings who had won twelve of the thirteen Grand Prix he contested was forced to settle for second with only four valuable points separating himself and his Red Bull KTM Factory Racing teammate Jordi Tixier. Tixier on the other hand used his ‘never give up’ attitude to silence his critics, bounce back from a dip and win his first ever FIM Motocross World Championship in impressive style.

It was expected to be epic and the 2015 FIM Motocross World Championship didn’t fail to deliver, in terms of being the best season of racing ever. Heading into round one, the MXGP of Qatar, all eyes were on Ryan Villopoto and the defending champ Antonio Cairoli. No one expected that neither Villopoto nor Cairoli would finish off the podium, and that is exactly what happened.

Red Bull IceOne Husqvarna Factory Racing’s Max Nagl was the early season dominator, he won both races in Qatar, had a shocker in Thailand but bounced back to finish inside the top two at the following four rounds. Nagl took the red plate at the MXGP of Trentino in Pietramurata, Italy, and held onto it for 6 rounds before breaking his ankle in the qualifying heat at round ten of the championship, his home grand prix, the MXGP of Germany. After Nagl’s disappointment in Thailand, it was the Rockstar Energy Suzuki World MXGP’s Clement Desalle who claimed the red plate and held it for three rounds before handing it over to Nagl.

Desalle’s season also came to an end early on when he dislocated his shoulder at round eight, the MXGP of France, which later required surgery. With both Nagl and Desalle on the sidelines, the breakout star of 2015, Yamaha Factory Racing Yamalube’s Romain Febvre took the red plate for the first time in his career. The series was fickle, but it was apparent that the rookie Romain Febvre was something special. The young Frenchman, youngest in the class to be precise, small in stature but with the potentially the greatest determination, clinched the red plate with both hands. He was crowned the 2015 FIM Motocross World Champion in the premier class MXGP with the statistics proving he is the worthy champion.

The ‘461’, Febvre’s number, led the most laps, 190 compared to Nagl’s 114. He won 15 races, 8 grand prix and finished on the podium 13 times in succession. Febvre wrapped up the championship at the sixteenth round of eighteen, in Assen, The Netherlands and finally won it by 143 points over fellow Frenchman, Team HRC’s Gautier Paulin. Paulin edged out his teammate Evgeny Bobryshev, from Russia, by 25 points. The Team HRC boys rounded off the podium in 2015.

Meanwhile in MX2, it was another dramatic year for the former two-times MX2 World Champion, Jeffrey Herlings. The Dutch sensation had some gnarly crashes throughout the season, which saw him miss round ten with a broken clavical and round twelve with a lacerated finger. The final time Herlings would line up in 2015 was at round thirteen, MXGP of The Czech Republic, where he broke his hip in qualifying and ended his season.

Despite the hardship of the number ‘84’, Herlings, he led the most laps, had the most race wins and held the red plate for the first 13 rounds of the championship. Due to his injury, the MX2 title was blown open, which saw the rise of Red Bull KTM Factory Racing’s Pauls Jonass, Honda Gariboldi’s young gun Tim Gajser, Monster Energy DRT Kawasaki’s Max Anstie and Standing Construct Yamaha Yamalube’s Valentin Guillod.

The fight was fierce. Max Anstie was the first rider to take a stand. The Brit took a double victory at the MXGP of Latvia and then again at the MXGP rounds of Belgium and Lombardia. He finished the season second on the leader board in terms of laps led, 147, behind Herlings who led 198 laps. Although Anstie had the speed, he didn’t have the consistency, especially that of Pauls Jonass, who held the red plate briefly despite that fact he hadn’t ever won a race. Jonass did manage to reach that milestone when he won his first ever MX2 race in Mexico, but by then Tim Gajser already had the red plate and was putting in some consistent results.

Nearing the end of the season, the pressure of potentially being crowned the 2015 FIM Motocross MX2 World Champion was starting to get to Jonass and Anstie. Both riders had some big crashes, which saw them throw away valuable points.

Gajser, on the other hand was riding solid. He took a surprise victory in the sand of Assen at MXGP of The Netherlands which made him top the charts as the rider with the most MX2 grand prix overalls, 5 compared to Herlings’ 4 and Guillod’s 3. A second place behind American Thomas Covington, who took his maiden MX2 race and grand prix win in Mexico, saw the Slovenian stretch his points lead ever so slightly.

The championship ended up going down to the wire with Gajser clinching the title in the final race of the season at the Monster Energy MXGP of The USA in Glen Helen. Jonass was forced to be content with second while Anstie rounded off the podium in third. With the newly crowned and unexpected MX2 champion of Tim Gajser joining the MXGP class, and the return of Jeffrey Herlings to MX2 racing, 2016 was set to be yet another season of extraordinary competition.

The 2015 MXGP World champion Romain Febvre and the 2015 MX2 champion Tim Gajser started the championship chase early at Qatar. To the surprise of many, Honda Gariboldi’s Gajser outperformed HRC Honda’s Evgeny Bobryshev and reigning champion Febvre. Gajser made it clear he was a title contender as a rookie but Romain Febvre answered by winning the following two rounds, capturing the red plate. Febvre held the points lead until Teutschenthal, where a tip over in the first moto gave the 243 Honda rider, Gajser, the edge he needed.

Multi-time champion Antonio Cairoli showed he was ready to compete after winning his first GP in nearly a year. The MXGP of Great Britain was an important point in the championship where the Yamaha rider of Febvre went down hard. Febvre was out of contention for two rounds allowing the points lead of Gajser to grow. Gajser rode consistent until Assen, where the conditions got the best of him. But with Febvre losing out on points the battle between 2<sup>nd</sup> and 3<sup>rd</sup> in the championship tightened. Afterwards, the championship headed to America. At the Monster Energy MXGP of Americas in Charlotte, North Carolina, Gajser was crowned FIM MXGP World Champion, only one year after winning the MX2 championship.

The season came to an end at Glen Helen where the American Eli Tomac won his second GP in a row. It was KTM’s Antonio Cairoli who finished the season 2<sup>nd</sup> followed by Husqvarna’s Max Nagl.

The first round of MX2 in Qatar started strong for title hopeful Dylan Ferrandis. Herlings claimed the first victory of the year going 1-1 with Ferrandis on his heals in both motos. Round 2 in Thailand saw the Kawasaki rider of Ferrandis dislocate his shoulder after going down in qualifying. Herlings capitalized and rode to another 1-1 finish. In moto 2, it was the hard charging Husqvarna rider Max Anstie finishing 2<sup>nd</sup> after crashing hard earlier in the day.

With Ferrandis injured Herlings continued his winning form going undefeated in his home race of Valkenswaard marking his 50<sup>th</sup> GP win. He continued to be undefeated the following 4 rounds. However, the MXGP of Trentino saw the number 4 of Ferrandis ready to challenge after returning to racing only one week before. After a hard fought battle Ferrandis handed Herlings his first loss of the year in moto 1. Seemingly unfazed the number 84 KTM of Herlings claimed the overall after a going 2-1 on the day and then won four more rounds before breaking his collar bone.

After missing the MXGPs’ of Loket, Belgium, and Switzerland due to his injury, Herlings finished the season strong to claim the FIM MX2 world championship at the first ever Monster Energy MXGP of Americas held on the dirt track of Charlotte Motor Speedway. Jeremy Seewer’s consistent season aboard his Suzuki secured him a well earned 2<sup>nd</sup> overall in the championship. Third place wasn’t decided until the final round at Monster Energy MXGP of USA, where Yamaha’s Benoit Paturel edged out the Englishman of Max Anstie.

**In 2017, the season started out at the all familiar circuit of Qatar, but there were some notable changes coming into the first few rounds of the new MXGP season. The biggest and most anticipated change was that of the 2016 FIM MX2 World Champion, Red Bull KTM Factory Racing’s Jeffrey Herlings moving up into the premier MXGP class. Aside from Herlings joining the field, Red Bull KTM Factory Racing’s Antonio Cairoli would start the season fully fit and healthy against a borage of competitors including the reigning FIM World Champion, Team HRC’s Tim Gajser and 2015 Champion Romain Febvre.**

When the gate dropped on the new mesh starting grid in Qatar Cairoli made it clear he was back to his winning ways as he took the first overall of the 2017 season all whilst battling with Gajser. For Jeffrey Herlings the opening round and the several following where a challenge while he healed from an off-season injury along with the realization that he had underestimated the depth and talent of the MXGP field.

A notable point of the 2017 season was that of the MXGP of Europe in Valkenswaard, Netherlands. While it was Rockstar Energy Husqvarna Factory Racing’s Gautier Paulin who took the overall win, much of the attention was instead on the resurgence of Jeffrey Herlings whom was finally starting to show the speed many assumed he was capable of as he finished 2<sup>nd</sup> .

One round later, at the MXGP of Latvia in Kegums, Herlings fans rejoiced as Jeffrey went on to take double victories for the overall win, the first of his MXGP career. While Herlings was on the rise it was the beginning of the issues for Gajser as he would suffer injury in a hard crash through the roller section. A stop later in Germany only added insult to the injury when Gajser cross rutted on a high-speed section of the track resulting in a DNF.

While Gajser healed, a new winner in 2017 surfaced by the likes of Monster Energy Kawasaki’s Clement Desalle. The Belgian took the MXGP of France overall by one point over Cairoli and quickly proved at the next stop, the MXGP of Russia, that it was no fluke as he again claimed an overall win.

The season then became a battle of giants between Antonio Cairoli and Jeffrey Herlings. The first epic battle came at the Fiat Professional FULLBACK MXGP of Lombardia where a game of cat and mouse ended in favor of Cairoli. 2 stops later at the MXGP of Belgium the duo again battled head to head but the determined Herlings topped the veteran of Cairoli.

Herlings would also win the MXGP of Switzerland Presented by iXS cutting down Cairoli’s points lead in the process. When the series headed to Sweden misfortune struck Herlings as he had a mechanical issue and lost points back to Cairoli. What was too little too late for Herlings provided to be just what the doctor ordered for Cairoli who went on to claim his 9<sup>th</sup> FIM Motocross World Championship at the MXGP of The Netherlands taking place in Assen.

In the MX2 category for 2017 Red Bull KTM Factory Racing’s Pauls Jonass returned from injury as the lead KTM MX2 rider with Herlings now aboard the 450 machine. Lining up alongside Jonass was a long list of competitors with massive talent from American Rockstar Energy Husqvarna Factory Racing’s Thomas Covington and Switzerland native and Suzuki World MX2’s Jeremy Seewer to young guns previously from the EMX250 category like Monster Energy DRT Kawasaki’s Darian Sanayei and Rockstar Energy Husqvarna Factory Racing’s Thomas Kjer Olsen.

To start the season Jonass won in Qatar while Seewer took the Indonesian victory. The MXGP of Trentino saw a new face stood on the top step of the podium, the young Spaniard Jorge Prado. Jonass would win next at Valkenswaard for the MXGP of Europe before heading to his home GP, the MXGP of Latvia. While hopes were high for Jonass to win at home the cards instead fell in favor of the EMX250 recruit and MX2 rookie Thomas Kjer Olsen.

After Latvia, Jonass gained traction and won 3 of the next six but Seewer did the same. As the title chase was close Jorge Prado claimed his 2<sup>nd</sup> overall of his career in Belgium. As the season headed towards a conclusion Jonass managed his points lead well as Seewer played catch-up. In the end the final round at Pays de Montbéliard decided the championship to the favor of Jonass.

**The 2018 Championship reached a new milestone for world motocross with an amazing schedule of 20 rounds kicking off at the stunning MXGP of Patagonia-Argentina with new contenders in the MXGP class such as Monster Energy Kawasaki Racing team’s Julien Lieber and 2017 MX2 Vice Champion Jeremy Seewer on a Wilvo Yamaha.**

While many where intrigued with the rookies almost all eyes were on defending champion Antonio Cairoli and his younger teammate Jeffrey Herlings. Herlings came into the season healthy proving his speed and determination against Cairoli in Neuquen to take the first red plate from a massive deficit with a last lap pass.

The pair continued to spar off as the season progressed with Cairoli taking the red plate at RedSand, Spain as Herlings took second overall. In the next seven rounds Herlings took 6 wins while Cairoli took none and Monster Energy Kawasaki Racing Team’s Clement Desalle took a lone victory in Russia.

The series was headed for Lombardia, Italy when news broke of Herlings injuring himself during training. The injury kept Herlings off the starting line in Italy and Cairoli took full advantage closing the points lead which had previously grown. A mere two weeks later Herlings was back on the starting line at the MXGP of Indonesia in Pangkal Pinang where he fought through pain and miraculously came away with the win while Cairoli injured his hand.

From Indonesia to Turkey Herlings was impeccable and took his first MXGP World Title at his home round in Assen for the MXGP of the Netherlands. With his family, fellow countrymen, and team Herlings made history taking the title at the conclusion of race win with a defiant win. Though already champion Herlings came back to the line and took another win for the overall. Perhaps the most dominant season of all time at the highest level of Motocross was topped off by Herlings with another double victory and overall win at the MXGP of Italy in Imola.

The 2018 MX2 season was in many ways comparable to that of the bigger MXGP class with the teammates of Pauls Jonass and Jorge Prado from Red Bull KTM fighting each other for the title. While the defending champion was Jonass, Prado was the young star on the rise.

At the beginning of the season Prado was recovering from a winter elbow injury but caught up to the pace of Jonass as soon as Valkenswaard in the Netherlands where the two first battled. Through the first three rounds Jonass was unbeatable with 6 race wins and 3 overall wins to match.

When the series landed in Trentino, Italy, however the young Spaniard split race wins with the American Thomas Covington on the way to his first win. In the next 16 races of the year Prado took 11 wins opposed to 2 from Jonass as 1 a piece went to Thomas Kjer Olsen, Calvin Vlaanderen, and Covington.

The season finale in Imola, Italy was set to host the championship deciding races but a knee injury to Jonass from a previous battle with Prado left the Latvian out of contention giving the gold plate and 2018 MX2 Championship title to the 17 year old Jorge Prado!

**2019 was another epic season, with incredible action in both MX2 and MXGP. The season once again kicked-off at the picturesque Argentina. Then the series headed back to Europe before making the trip across to Asia for two Indonesian rounds, with the championship concluding in Shanghai, China.**

In MX2 Spaniard, Jorge Prado was dominant as he took 16 GP wins out of 18 and a total of 31 race wins, having missed the British GP due to a shoulder injury. This allowed the Rockstar Energy Husqvarna rider, Thomas Kjer Olsen to take the red plate and hold on to it for a few rounds in the beginning of the season, but of course Prado was crowned the MX2 World Champion in 2019. His team-mate Tom Vialle had a fantastic rookie season as he finished fourth in the championship standings, missing out on the podium to Jago Geerts by just six points.

In MXGP fans were treated to some of the most epic battles in the history of MXGP, as we watched Tim Gajser and the 9-time World Champion, Antonio Cairoli in combat at almost every round in the beginning of the season, while Jeffrey Herlings was side-lined with a foot injury sustained in pre-season training.

One of the highlights of the season came from the MXGP of Trentino in Italy, as Gajser and Cairoli race wheel-to-wheel from start to finish, treating fans to a nail-biting race. Gajser won race one by just 01.081 seconds, while in race two saw Cairoli crash out on the last lap, handing Gajser a comfortable win.

As Herlings made his comeback, in Latvia he was struck with some more bad luck after a fall in the opening race sighting lap left him with a broken foot. Not aware of the extent of the injury Herlings still lined-up and went on to win the opening race, following a battle with Arnaud Tonus.

Disaster also struck Cairoli after a crash left him with a dislocated shoulder and would mean he would have to miss the remainder of the 2019 season, putting his hopes of a 10<sup>th</sup> world title on hold.

Gajser won a total of nine GP’s and 16 races, going on to claim his 3<sup>rd</sup> World Title, his second in MXGP. Second was Jeremy Seewer and in third was Standing Construct KTM’s, Glenn Coldenhoff who finished the season on a high with 4 race wins and 2 Grand Prix victories.

The year concluded at the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations as Glenn Coldenhoff, Jeffrey Herlings and Calvin Vlaanderen made history, going on to dominate the world and putting the Netherlands in the history of winners, as they won the Chamberlain Trophy in Assen in front of their home crowd.

# **2020-2021**

**The 2020 and 2021 seasons came with entirely new set of challenges, not only for the promotor, the organisers, the FIM, teams and riders – but the entire world – as we all lived through the COVID-19 pandemic. These two years saw the world come to a stop – as did sporting events, meaning that the FIM Motocross World Championship was put on hold, and once it did get going again, it was with no public and under strict restrictions that required protocols, and a strict set of rules and regulations to continue racing.**

In the beginning of the 2020 season, things were looking positive as the series kicked off in Matterley Basin, the host of the British Grand Prix. It was the start of an exciting year with one of the strongest line-ups in both MXGP and MX2 categories to date. Jeffrey Herlings began the season with an overall victory in Great Britain and then followed that up with another win on home soil in Valkenswaard, after sharing the race wins with Tim Gajser. Herlings held the red plate into the third round.

The plan was to head to Patagonia-Argentina next, though as the COVID-19 pandemic progressed, the Argentinian GP, along with numerous other rounds were forced to be re-scheduled or even cancelled in some cases, which put the FIM Motocross World Championship on hold for five whole months!

Much to everyone’s avail, racing finally returned in August with the first ever triple-header event in Kegums, Latvia. The Latvian trio of races introduced another exciting aspect, a mid-week Grand Prix, which was the only way to follow restrictions and still allow for a full championship.

The MXGP of Latvia, Riga and Kegums proved hard for the series leader, as Herlings struggled with strong results, though still managed two podiums out of three, including an overall win at the third and final event (MXGP of Kegums). The three GPs in Kegums also saw some new winners, with Glenn Coldenhoff and the Standing Construct GASGAS Factory Team taking their first overall victory of the season during the MXGP of Latvia, while Antonio Cairoli made his return to the top step of the podium during the MXGP of Riga and Arminas Jasikonis secured his maiden race win in the premier class during the MXGP of Riga too. For Gajser the Latvian races were tough, though he managed some race wins, while the two DNF’s made things tough in terms of the championship points.

Faenza was the second triple-header of 2020 that saw Herlings secure his first 1-1 of the season, while during the second of the three GPs, Monster Energy Yamaha Factory Racing’s Jeremy Seewer got his maiden race victory in the premier class, while Red Bull KTM Factory Racing’s Jorge Prado showed that consistency can pay off, as he secured the overall win with a 2-2 result over Gajser and Seewer.

The mid-week GP in Italy saw Herlings being dealt a big blow to his championship hopes, as the Bullet was forced out of the rest of the year due to injury, following a crash in the free practice session. By the following Grand Prix (MXGP of Emilia Romagna) Prado was able to secure his first race win ahead of Cairoli and Seewer. Though Cairoli being the most consistent rider that time around, with two solid second place finishes, was the overall winner and with that became the new championship leader by the end of the three races in Faenza.

Cairoli’s time in the lead was cut short as after the MXGP of Lombardia, Tim Gajser took over. The very same weekend, Seewer secured his very first Grand Prix win in MXGP. The MXGP of Città di Mantova was the weekend of Monster Energy Kawasaki Racing’s Romain Febvre who took to the top step of the podium. As we finished our trip in Mantova, it was Gajser who took the final Grand Prix win in Italy, before the series moved to Spain, visiting the brandnew intu Xanadú – Arroyomolinos circuit. Feeling right at home, it was Jorge Prado who dominated that weekend, with two solid race wins on home soil in his MXGP rookie season.

Next was the Belgian triple-header in Lommel, that saw Tim Gajser victorious during the Monster Energy MXGP of Flanders and MXGP of Lommel, while Jorge Prado got the win at the MXGP of Limburg, before his season was cut short due to COVID-19.

The last three rounds in Pietramurata, Italy, were exciting to say the least. Antonio Cairoli was able to come back with a bang to claim the first overall victory in Trentino, following a great battle with Clement Desalle, with the Belgian adding a race win to his season tally. The final two rounds saw the domination of Tim Gajser who finished his year with 5 GP wins, 14 podiums and 15 race wins to become the 2020 MXGP World Champion. Vice World Champion for the year was Jeremy Seewer with 6 podiums, 3 race wins and a GP victory. Meanwhile Antonio Cairoli was forced to settle for a bronze medal.

In MX2, the title chase was just as exciting. With an impressive 14 race wins, 14 GP podiums, and 7 Grand Prix victories in his back pocket, Red Bull KTM Factory Racing’s Tom Vialle secured his maiden MX2 World Title, following a season long battle with Jago Geerts of Monster Energy Yamaha Factory Racing. The Belgian was a strong competitor and in terms of stats was not far behind Vialle, with 14 race wins, 12 Grand Prix podiums and 6 GP victories. Things were close between the pair as they often exchanged race wins, though mistakes proved costly for Geerts who often struggled to keep it on two wheels. Finishing third in the MX2 Championship standings was Maxime Renaux. Renaux showed strong form in 2020, making an appearance on the podium a total of 5 times, which includes his overall victory at the MXGP of Città di Faenza.

While everyone had hoped to conclude the year with the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations, unfortunately COVID-19 restrictions did not allow for the most anticipated event of the season to go ahead, though the MXoN did return in 2021.

# **2021**

**By the 2021 season, restrictions remained tight, though in some parts of the world were becoming more relaxed as the vaccination campaigns were introduced and the numbers decreased. This as a result slowly allowed fans to return to races – though again, following strict protocols.**

Though the beginning of the season was pushed back on several occasions, but eventually began in Orlyonok where Tim Gajser asserted his dominance as the defending World Champion from the get-go, winning race one and making an impressive comeback in race two to secure the GP victory and taking the red plate. The opening Grand Prix was a difficult one for Antonio Cairoli who suffered a DNF, while Jeffrey Herlings was still coming back to strength after injury in 2020.

The MXGP action at the season opener was exciting with where Tim Gajser assertingasserted his dominance as the defending World Champion from the get-go, winning race one and making an impressive come-back in race two to secure the GP victory and taking the red plate. The opening Grand Prix was a difficult one for Antonio Cairoli who suffered a DNF, while Jeffrey Herlings was still coming back to strength after injury in 2020.

By the second round we saw Cairoli bounce back with an overall victory, while Gajser finished second, still maintaining the championship lead ahead over Herlings. Herlings then took his first GP win of the season in Maggiora after tying on points with Glenn Coldenhoff and Cairoli, as Gajser and Febvre struggled in the muddy conditions. Febvre did win race one but crashes in race two, made it difficult for the Frenchman to get on the podium.

The GP in Oss (Netherlands) was next, a new circuit to the calendar. The MXGP Championship took an unexpected turn, as a start-device failure saw Herlings end his race with a shoulder injury after being landed on by Ivo Monticelli, who could not avoid ‘the Bullet’. Powered by sheer adrenaline he was able to finish the race and even win it, but did not line up for race two. Gajser took the overall, with Cairoli making it back-to-back podiums, while Febvre was third.

Herlings missed out on the MXGP of Czech Republic that once again dealt another shock in the series. Jorge Prado went on to win the GP, while Gajser lost a lot of points after crashing in race two and only managing to come back to 15<sup>th</sup> . Cairoli and Jeremy Seewer joined the Spaniard on the podium.

Then the action headed to the deep sand of Lommel, with Herlings making a shock return just two weeks after injury. Herlings and Febvre battled closely in race one, with the Dutchman ultimately taking the victory, while in race two numerous crashes proved to be costly, while Febvre was able to get his head down. This was the first GP win of the year for Febvre. Herlings finished second, while Pauls Jonass made his first podium appearance since his injury in 2020 in third.

The Latvian Grand Prix saw Gajser back on the top step, with Prado on the box once again as Herlings finished third and continued his journey back to the top. At the Bitci MXGP of Turkey we saw Herlings back on the top step with a back-to-back win during both the Turkish races.

**143**

## Pagina 144

MXGP HISTORY **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

Next came Sardegna and with heavy rumours flying around about a potential injury from one of the toprunners, this was confirmed, as Honda announced a practice injury for Gajser. Gajser despite trying his best was unable to perform, while Antonio Cairoli had to retire from the GP due to a crash in time practice and Herlings made the most to go 1-1 in the deep sand and took over the championship lead by just a single point.

Gajser was able to bounce back in Germany with a GP win, as well as taking back the red plate, while Prado and Herlings went head-to-head in a dramatic end to the first race, as the pair collided on the finish line. Herlings was able to walk away and start race two, while Prado did not and received medical attention.

Next up was the MXGP of France as we headed to Lacapelle Marival. There we saw the comeback of the fans, which made for an incredible atmosphere as Febvre and Herlings fought for the win. The pair shared a race win each, but with Herlings winning the second heat, it was him who stood on the top step of the podium. Herlings took back the championship lead from Gajser, who struggled, while Febvre closed in and moved into second in the standings.

The Spanish Grand Prix then saw a huge fan turnout – which was just incredible. Looking to put his best foot forward and bounce back after a couple of tough races, home hero Jorge Prado was able to do just that to finish second on the box. His battles with Herlings, Febvre and Gajser were close, but it was ultimately Herlings who won the GP despite Gajser’s and Febvre’s best efforts.

By the triple-header in the Dolomites mountains of Trentino, the title chase had reached boiling point. At the first GP (MXGP of Trentino) Herlings was unbeatable going 1-1 and started to break away in the championship standings. Coldenhoff got himself on the podium for the second time this season and was not going to let the heat of the title fight affect his personal results, while Gajser was able to finish his weekend on the box again.

While it looked like Herlings could finally gain control of the championship, things to a 180 turn, as a crash in the start of the opening race left the Bullets bike too damaged to carry on which meant that he would DNF the race. This was a gift to Febvre and Gajser who were keen on maximising their points. Herlings was able to salvage some points in race two and by the end of the GP we saw Herlings, Febvre and Gajser separated by just three points. And while many were focused on the title, Cairoli gave the fans something more to cheer about as he got on the top step of the podium at his home race.

By the time of the final race in Pietramurata (MXGP of Garda) everybody knew this GP could set the course for the rest of the races left on the calendar. While everyone looked for Herlings to bounce back, the result was quite different as a few big crashes proved to be costly. Herlings failed to mount the podium, as Seewer claimed his second career GP win with Gajser second and Febvre third. Febvre’s solid results also handed him the red plate for the first time in 2021, as the championship remained tight with just three points between the #3, #243 and #84.

And then it was time for the title showdown as the final two races of the season took place in Mantova, Italy. While the previous two races may have given the impression that Herlings was starting to crack, he proved otherwise as he hit back with a 2-1 result for the GP win. Febvre was second with a similar result 2-1. But the main topic of the GP was Gajser penalty after the mishap out of the start. Gajser’s penalty pretty much took him out of the title fight, and we were down to a two-man race at the MXGP of Città di Mantova.

Febvre entered the final race of the season 3 points ahead of his closest rival Herlings. Herlings was able to put together a strong first race to go on a win, which meant that the Bullet and the Frenchman were tied on points going into the second race. Things were looking ok on the first lap of race two, as Febvre led the way but Herlings was able to get around him and while the Kawasaki rider looked to respond, a small mistake which resulted in a crash was the ultimate decider.

Gajser was right there to get by Febvre as he picked himself up and then applied the pressure onto the leader, who was trying his best to hold on despite severe arm-pump. Herlings was able to bring it home to go 1-1, secure his fifth world title and with the Grand Prix too!

Gajser was second overall on the day, while Febvre was third. The MXGP Championship standings looked like this by the chequered flag: Herlings with 708 points, Febvre on 703 and Gajser with 688.

In MX2, Frenchman Maxime Renaux got his hands on his first ever title after an injury early in the season took out Tom Vialle’s chances of defending his crown.

Of course, also this season we saw the return of the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations which took place around the mid-way of the season. The re-scheduled 74<sup>th</sup> edition delivered some exciting races at the Mantova circuit, with Team Italy’s Antonio Cairoli, Alessandro Lupino and Mattia Guadagnini winning the Chamberlain Trophy in front of their home fans! Team Netherlands were forced to settle for 2<sup>nd</sup> , while Team Great Britain were 3<sup>rd</sup> for the 4<sup>th</sup> consecutive time.

# **2022**

**The 2022 season saw the world at the FIM Motocross World Championship return to ‘normality’ with the weekend format back to the usual two-day racing. While a few restrictions remained in place, the progress of the pandemic allowed the return for the overseas GP’s in Argentina and Indonesia, as well as the comeback of some favourites in Sweden and Finland, among others.**
