MXGP HISTORY **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

The season kicked off, a week later than planned, due to weather conditions in Matterley Basin for the British GP. With some of the key players not lining up, for example Cairoli’s retirement and the injuries of Herlings and Febvre, the focus was on Gajser and Prado as the pair seemed to be the front-runners of the group, though rookie Maxime Renaux of Monster Energy Yamaha factory Racing was there to put up a fight after winning the qualifying heat.

Gajser dominated the first three GPs before Prado was able to score his first visit to the top step in Portugal, as Brian Bogers of Standing Construct Husqvarna Factory Racing secured his very first MXGP podium!

The action then moved on to Trentino where Gajser was able to claim his fourth GP win of the season, he then continued his run with another win at the following round in Latvia, while home hero Pauls Jonass celebrated a podium finish on home soil as he placed second ahead of Ruben Fernandez who got his first taste of the MXGP podium on his rookie season.

In Maggiora, Gajser continued his positive run with another GP win ahead of Seewer and Renaux. It all looked like Gajser was going to dominate the rest of the season, an illness ended up taking a toll on the Slovenian who struggled for the next three rounds. That’s when some new names got their turn on the top step of the podium. Starting with Sardegna which was dominated by Calvin Vlaanderen who was not only the fastest in timed practice but won the qualifying heat and both races! It was a huge moment for Vlaanderen who celebrated his first MXGP podium – which just so happened to be a GP victory!

Sweden saw Seewer top the Grand Prix while in Finland Yamaha celebrated an incredible result as the first blue 1-2-3 with Coldenhoff on top while Seewer and Renaux finished second and third. But of course, this was also the same weekend that Gajser wrapped up his fifth world title, despite failing to mount the podium.

The final two GPs were also won by Gajser, who without the pressure of wrapping up the title could focus on enjoying the races. But the final two GPs of the year in Saint Jean d’Angely and Afyon were the focus of the title showdown in MX2 between Tom Vialle and Jago Geerts. After watching the pair battle closely for the entire season, that saw both riders suffer with bad luck, for example Vialle’s huge crash in Mantova, Geerts’ DNF in Trentino and Vialle’s DNF in Germany. Both had to fight hard and the outcome was incredible from a spectators’ point of view.

After the bad luck for Geerts in Finland, the Championship opened up again as Vialle took to the top step. After that it seemed like the confidence gained was huge for Vialle who went on to win his home GP at the MXGP of Charente Maritime ahead of Thibault Benistant and Geerts and subsequently winning the final GP in Turkiye and clinching the title by just four points after an intense final heat that saw both riders crash together!

The 2022 season was then concluded with the 75<sup>th</sup> edition of the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations which returned to RedBud for the first time since 2018! It was a weekend of redemption for Team USA as Eli Tomac, Justin Cooper and Chase Sexton ended America’s 11-year winless streak by putting the American flag on the top step on home soil.

# **2023**

In Spain, Maxime Renaux was the overall winner for the first time in the MXGP category ahead of Glenn Coldenhoff and Brian Bogers, as home rider Jorge Prado struggled. In Ernée, Prado bounced back with second overall behind Jeremy Seewer who got his first GP win of the season, while his teammate Coldenhoff place third after winning the second race.

At the Liqui Moly MXGP of Germany, Gajser bounced back with a win as we also saw the return of Romain Febvre. This was all before we packed up and made our way to Indonesia for a first GP in Samota-Sumbawa.

The venue in Sumbawa was impressive and the track received very positive reviews from the riders! Though the Indonesian round came with its own challenges for many on the grid as Gajser continued to lead the way.

In Czech Republic, Seewer was the man to beat as he dominated the qualifying and then went 1-2 to take his second GP win of the season. The Flanders Grand Prix in Lommel saw some more surprises! Gajser struggled as his fifth world title was looming. That time around we were treated to an impressive Dutch showdown as the final race saw Bogers, Vlaanderen and Coldenhoff going at it for the top step. In the end, it was Bogers who came out on top as Vlaanderen placed second ahead of Coldenhoff.

**The 2023 season of the FIM Motocross World Championship marked a significant return to normalcy, as it was the first season since the COVID-19 pandemic without any restrictive measures. This news brought immense joy to fans worldwide, especially those eagerly anticipating the overseas races.**

The season's journey began in Argentina-Patagonia, although without defending champion Tim Gajser, who suffered a pre-season injury. However, the five-time World Champion Jeffrey Herlings made an anticipated comeback after a year-long injury. It was Jorge Prado who capitalised on the newly implemented point system for the RAM Qualifying Races. While Ruben Fernandez clinched the first Grand Prix of the season and his career, the Red Plate was claimed by Jorge Prado from the outset. Herlings, making his return, secured his first podium of the season.

It didn't take long for Herlings to reassert his dominance, as he staged an extraordinary comeback in Sardinia, climbing from 14<sup>th</sup> to 2<sup>nd</sup> place in a spectacular display. On the sandy terrain of Riola Sardo, Herlings surpassed nearly all his competitors, except Glenn Coldenhoff, who won his first race of the season. Herlings seemed on a mission to reach his 100<sup>th</sup> GP victory and inch closer to Stefan Everts' remarkable record of 102 single GP wins.

**146**

## Pagina 147

MXGP HISTORY **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

The third Grand Prix in Switzerland witnessed Maxime Renaux's first Grand Prix victory, signifying his strong contention for the championship. Jorge Prado retained the Red Plate after triumphing in the first three RAM Qualifying Races and clinching victory in 50% of the Sunday races over the first six GPs. His dominance became even more apparent when he secured his first Grand Prix win of the season in Trentino.

The Red Plate faced pressure with Herlings going all out in the next two GPs, first in Portugal, where he equalled Everts' record with 101 GP wins. Herlings' form made it seem like the long-standing record was on the verge of falling. Romain Febvre reached the podium for the first time that season, taking 2<sup>nd</sup> place, while Prado maintained his 100% podium record since the season's start.

The MXGP of Spain followed, where Herlings was determined to outclass his rivals. He accomplished a perfect 1-1, securing his ultimate record of 102 GP wins. Prado missed his first podium of the season, and the tides were turning.

The competition reached new heights in Villars sous Ecot for the MXGP of France, where Jeremy Seewer made a triumphant comeback to the top step of the podium. The Swiss rider was not ready to let Prado and Herlings pull away in the Championship. However, Herlings was on a remarkable rise, closing in on Prado, who led by just 15 points.

The LiquiMoly MXGP of Germany was a turning point in the Championship, with Herlings challenging and overtaking Prado in Race 1. The crowd was on the edge of their seats when Herlings suffered a severe crash, allowing Prado to respond with a perfect 1-1 victory.

Herlings was out of the title race as the Championship moved to Indonesia for a double-header. Prado had a path ahead to secure his lead. However, Febvre's outstanding performance, winning five Grand Prix in a row, posed a significant challenge to Prado. While Febvre collected Grand Prix wins, Prado consistently accumulated race wins and RAM Qualifying Races victories, maintaining his advantage in the standings.

The closest rivals, Seewer and Febvre, won two and one Grand Prix, respectively, but couldn't catch up to Prado's remarkable consistency. A notable moment of the season was Tim Gajser's comeback, who secured victory in Turkiye, exactly one year after his last win. He repeated this feat in the last Grand Prix of the season in Great Britain at Matterly Basin, ending the season on a high note.

It was only a matter of time before Prado was mathematically crowned MXGP World Champion, which occurred at the MXGP of Italy in Maggiora. This eventful Grand Prix saw Febvre's bike fail under him in Race 1, allowing Prado to secure the race win and his first MXGP World Title with a Grand Prix to spare.

The same Grand Prix also saw Andrea Adamo crowned in MX2, displaying relentless consistency throughout the season and demonstrating great resilience. Jago Geerts initially held the Red Plate but got injured in France, paving the way briefly to Kay de Wolf and then Adamo. The Italian maintained his leadership, even when Geerts made a late-season charge. In the end, in Maggiora with his 10<sup>th</sup> podium of the season, Adamo finally became MX2 World Champion, thanks to his unwavering consistency.

The 2023 season culminated with the 76<sup>th</sup> edition of the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations, returning to Ernée, France, following the resounding success of the 2015 edition. This unforgettable weekend witnessed the largest crowd ever gathered at the 'Nations'. Team France, competing on their home turf, delivered an exceptional team performance, clinching their 7<sup>th</sup> MXoN title in history. The unique and incredible atmosphere was palpable as Romain Febvre, Maxime Renaux, and Tom Vialle each played a crucial role in securing the victory.

# **2024-2025**

**The 2024 season treated us to two Championships that were closely contested throughout the 20-round MXGP schedule, as the series said farewell to the Villa La Angostura venue with another thrilling season opener, but welcomed new circuits in Lugo and Cozar, as well as returning to Shanghai for The Oriental Beauty Valley MXGP of China, a round that proved decisive in the MXGP World Championship!**

The MXGP class saw a year-long tussle between multiple World Champions Jeffrey Herlings, Tim Gajser, and Jorge Prado, with the momentum swinging one way then the other between the three titans, who each took memorable GP wins in the process. Each suffered their issues, with Herlings losing a potential race win to a mechanical failure in the deep mud of Portugal, a round won by former MX2 Champ Pauls Jonass, the only 2024 GP winner apart from the big three!

Prado got caught up in a big first lap crash at Maggiora, causing enough pain to give him a nonscore and a gap to chase back on Gajser through the second half of the year. The Slovenian’s misfortunes had the worst timing, however, with crashes and a broken footpeg hitting him in the penultimate round at Shanghai, handing the red plate back to Prado with a round to go! The Spaniard dutifully delivered his 11<sup>th</sup> Grand Prix win of the year on home soil at Cozar to clinch his second consecutive MXGP world crown for Red Bull GASGAS Factory Racing.

The MX2 series was a two-horse race between the Nestaan Husqvarna Factory Racing teammates, Kay de Wolf and Lucas Coenen, and as the Dutchman De Wolf held the red plate from the end of round one, setting the pace with the first three GP victories and seven for the year, Coenen gave chase all season long with his relentless speed.

**147**

## Pagina 148

MXGP HISTORY **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

Liam Everts took two victories in mid-season, and Sacha Coenen took the first two GP wins of his career, meaning that the Coenen brothers became the first twins ever to both win Motocross Grands Prix. It all came down to the final race at the MXGP of Castilla La Mancha, with Coenen taking his 9<sup>th</sup> GP win, but De Wolf doing all that was needed to seal his first World Championship on his 20<sup>th</sup> birthday, and the first for the Husqvarna brand in 25 years!

The season finished in fine style with the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations at Matterley Basin in the UK, and amongst some incredible racing action, Team Australia took their first ever victory in the event. Jett Lawrence won the MX Open class and was ably backed up by his brother Hunter, and MX2 rider Kyle Webster. Kay de Wolf was the MX2 class winner to crown his amazing season, but Tim Gajser was the star of the day with two wins in the MXGP class, including a penultimate corner pass on Jett Lawrence that finished the year with Motocross fans screaming their heads off!

The Behind The Gate series continued to go from strength to strength, profiling the riders and teams behind the sport, and Inside the Paddock returned at the MXoN with new presenter Ben Rumbold, showing a mix between the crazy fans and unique team arrangements at the MXoN.

**2025 gave us an enthralling pair of World Championship fights as the 20-round series visited four continents, and a fifth one for the Motocross of Nations! Finland returned to the calendar at the new KymiRing venue, but the biggest excitement was for the return of MXGP to the land Down Under, as the city of Darwin hosted the Motocross World Championship’s first visit to Australia in nearly a quarter of a century! The Hidden Valley Motorsports Complex would be the venue for what turned out to be a memorable Championship finale.**

MXGP sadly started the year without Jeffrey Herlings, who had injured his knee just after the 2024 MXoN, and reigning Champion Jorge Prado who opted to race elsewhere. Yamaha started the year strongly with Maxime Renaux taking the opening GP victory at the spectacular new Argentine venue of Córdoba. Winning three of the next four GPs, however, was HRC Honda’s Tim Gajser, who became only the sixth rider in history to win a 50<sup>th</sup> career Motocross Grand Prix, in the deep mud of Cozar in Spain.

Looking strong with the red plate after the first five GPs, the Slovenian crashed in Switzerland, resulting in a shoulder injury that unfortunately ended his title run. Swiss veteran Jeremy Seewer gave Ducati their first podium in his homeland, but that GP, and the one after it in Portugal, was won by Belgian class rookie Lucas Coenen, who became the youngest ever overall winner in the MXGP category at under 18 and a half years old!

The red plate was taken by Romain Febvre, the factory Kawasaki man looking strong with overall victory at Lugo in Spain, his first ever win in that country, then in front of his adoring home fans at Ernée, where he made the valley shake with a brilliant pass on Coenen in the closing stages!

Jeffrey Herlings had joined the series at round four, and started to find his speed again, winning GPs in Germany and, for the ninth time in his career, at Kegums in Latvia, but his fellow Red Bull KTM pilot Coenen held a run of five second place overall results to maintain his title challenge, then won three in a row in Finland, Czechia, and triumphantly on home ground at Lommel! However, Febvre took 14 consecutive podium finishes to keep his grip on the red plate, while Glenn Coldenhoff held third in the series to the end of the season, giving Fantic their first race win as a manufacturer at Matterley Basin.

Febvre’s podium run concluded with a perfect weekend in Sweden, but that GP will be remembered as one of the most emotional ever as home hero Isak Gifting, roared on by his Viking-hat-wearing fans, sensationally passed both Herlings and Febvre in the final two laps to take the lead! To the despair of everyone watching, he then dropped the bike in a corner and was unable to restart. Tears flowed everywhere as he was forced to walk back to the paddock.

The Dutch GP followed, and Herlings amazingly took a record 16<sup>th</sup> win on home sand despite being pushed harder than ever by Lucas Coenen. “The Bullet” took victory in Türkiye and China, too, but Coenen had a nightmare in the oriental heat with 12<sup>th</sup> overall. This allowed Febvre to clinch the world title in Australia, an incredible ten years on from his first world crown in 2015. No rider in history has had to wait so long between their first two titles, and for Kawasaki it was an even longer wait, taking their first premier class title since 1998!

The MX2 series was a wide open contest for most of the season, with the red plate being passed around at first between Andrea Adamo, Sacha Coenen, reigning Champion Kay de Wolf and his new Nestaan Husqvarna teammate Liam Everts, before Red Bull KTM’s Simon Längenfelder started to put things together, finally breaking a 20-month GP win drought with a perfect weekend in Switzerland.

Injury niggles for De Wolf hampered his title defence slightly, and we saw the first race wins for Triumph as a manufacturer with Camden McLellan and Guillem Farres taking the chequers in Trentino and Germany. Former Champ Adamo took four GP wins in the first ten rounds to lead the series at half-distance. However, Längenfelder and De Wolf traded the GP wins from rounds 12 to 18 to leave the Italian firmly in bronze medal position, with Sacha Coenen fourth following victories in Latvia and China.

**148**

## Pagina 149

MXGP HISTORY **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

It all came down to a mighty tussle in the heat, and then the pouring rain, of Australia! De Wolf needed to close in a 13-point margin on the final day, and did all he could to stop his German rival, but as the final race was met by monsoon conditions it was the Dutchman who suffered a drowning motor as the rain called an early halt to proceedings. De Wolf took the GP win as the laps were counted back, but Simon Längenfelder, after three straight bronze medals, held his nerve to become the first German World Champion for 14 years, celebrated in style with a fine dive into the mud!

The Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations returned to the USA at a new circuit for the event, Ironman Raceway near Crawfordsville, Indiana. The biggest Team Presentation event ever seen for the Nations took over the town centre at Crawfordsville with a classic American street parade that blew everybody’s minds! The atmosphere was at fever pitch as ever, but the result literally stayed the same, as Team Australia defended their title with the same trio as in 2024. The only difference was that it was Hunter Lawrence who went through the event unbeaten, with his brother Jett finishing a mere 1-3 in his races! With Kyle Webster again proving ample backup on the MX2 bike, it was actually the first time ever in the three-man Nations format’s history that the exact same riders had won the team title for two years running! The event moves back to Ernée in France for what’s sure to be a monumental weekend in 2026!

The Behind The Gate series continued to impress with insight from MXGP Commentator Paul Malin, MXGP Presenter Lisa Leyland, and journalist Ben Rumbold, with the riders and teams allowing the crew further into their world than ever before, and the Inside the Paddock feature also delivered more craziness from the MXoN! With Paul and US counterpart Jason Thomas adding more analysis with the MXGP Podcast and Lisa’s continued online Paddock Talk series, MXGP fans received more access than ever before into the inner workings of the top of the sport!

REGULATIONS **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

# 2026 FIM MXGP / MX2 REGULATIONS

## **SHORT VERSION OF THE 2026 FIM MXGP/MX2 MOTOCROSS WORLD CHAMPIONSHIP REGULATIONS**

### **GENERAL**

- MXGP: Minimum age of riders: 16 years

- MX2: Minimum age of riders: 15 years

- The limit for the minimum age starts on the rider’s birthday and must be reached at the moment of the first technical verifications of the event in question.

- The limit for the maximum age finishes at the end of the calendar year in which the rider reaches the maximum age of the class in question. The maximum age limit of 23 years for riders in the MX2 class does not apply to women riders. Riders who have won two FIM MX2 World Championships or reached the age of 23 are no longer eligible to participate in the MX2 class.

- Riders must have an FIM World Championship licence.

- After the Qualifying Race on Saturday, the points leader must display the front red number plate with white numbers during the two Races on Sunday.

- The Championship points gained during the Qualifying Race do not count towards the event’s final standings.

- Rider’s apparel: Riders must wear helmets, eye protection, and unmodified, full upper-body protection (front and back) in accordance with the FIM Motocross Technical Rules. From 2026, only helmets conforming to the FIM FRHPhe-02 standard will be permitted. Self-certified airbags are permitted.

- Riders cannot groom their starting position. They must visually select their starting gate position from behind the rear barrier and take the start from a metal starting platform provided by the Championship Promoter.

- “Goggle Exchange Lane”: in this area the team member may give assistance to the rider to clean his face/eyes and change goggles/gloves. Any other assistance in this area is prohibited.

- Any outside assistance on the course to a rider is forbidden unless it is provided by a marshal, a FIM official or FMNR official in the interests of safety. However, marshals, FIM officials and FMNR officials are not allowed to hold a rider’s motorcycle or to help a rider re-start his motorcycle.

- Stopping of race +2 laps and 51% race completed: there will be a restart from the gate with riders taking their position behind the starting gate according to their finishing order at the end of the lap preceding the stopping of the race. Riders will be released by an official from the gate, one by one

- Motorcycles and classes: MXGP 2-stroke engines + 175cc up to 250cc and 4-stroke engines + 290ccup to 450cc

- MX2 2-stroke engines + 100cc up to 125cc and 4-stroke engines + 175cc up to 250cc

- All permanent MXGP and MX2 Teams (OAT’s) must equip their motorcycles with and use the official compulsory Promoter/FIM GPS and RPM electronic system at all times during official sessions (including all practices and races).

- Bodies at an FIM World Championship: Event Management, Race Direction, FIM Stewards Panel.

- Riders can use 2 motorcycles during the weekend which have been controlled at technical verification. After the sighting lap, a rider can change motorcycle as long as he has not taken his position behind the starting gate  (= has not crossed the rear barrier behind the starting gate with his motorcycle yet).

- When crossing the finish, riders must continue at **race speed until they have passed the “End Finish Zone” sign** and clear the finish area as quick as possible. Slowing down abruptly or stopping in the area is not allowed unless it is ordered by an official.

- Sound control by 2mMax sound test:

- The maximum limit of the sound level pre-race technical verifications at full rpm is fixed at: For all engine types: **111.0 dB/A (measured with a Type 1 or Type 2 sound meter) (109.0 dB/A + 2.0 dB/A for the precision of the method)**

- The maximum limit of the sound level post-race technical verifications at full rpm is fixed at: For all engine types:

- **112.0 dB/A (measured with a Type 1 or Type 2 sound meter) (111.0 dB/A + 1.0 dB/A for the precision of the method)**

- Gradual penalties for non-conformity with post race the sound limits:

- First offence drop of 2 positions

- Second offence drop of 5 positions

MXoN HISTORY **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

# **LOOKING BACK ON THE YEARS OF THE LEGENDARY MONSTER ENERGY FIM MOTOCROSS OF NATIONS!**

## **BRITISH DOMINANCE**

**Motocross, known as “scramble”, when it was first introduced in the 1920’s, developed quickly in the 30’s. Following the war, the FIM elevated the sport at international level by presenting the Motocross des Nations (official title in French, run with 500cc motorcycles), a classic event which has been run annually ever since.**

The introductory event was held near the Hague, in the Netherlands on July 20<sup>th</sup> of 1947. Three nations were invited to take part – the Netherlands, Belgium and Great Britain. The Belgian rider Auguste Mingels was the fastest, but the A team of Great Britain with Bill Nicholson (BSA), Fred Rist (BSA) and Bob Ray (Ariel) won the first team title, ahead of the Belgian “B” squad.

The second “Nations” contest took place in August 1948 in La Fraineuse, close to the Belgian city of Spa. National federations of France, Sweden and Luxembourg were also invited for an event dominated by the local team. Nic Jansen, Marcel Cox and Andre Milhoux took first, second and fifth places, clinching the first Trophy for Belgium.

At the end of August 1949, it was time for Great Britain to host the final competition of the decade at the famous Brands Hatch circuit. The British team won the competition for the second time, thus keeping the Cup indefinitely. The ACU then donated another Cup for the competition’s winning team. The Vice President of the FIM International Sporting Commission Peter Chamberlain had always been a strong supporter of Motocross and worked a great deal on behalf of the national teams’ competition. After he passed away in 1954, the cup was officially named after him and became known as the ‘Chamberlain Trophy’.

In the 50s, the British dominance was quite strong as the team clinched seven out of a possible 10 victories in the Motocross des Nations before 1960. The only exceptions were the victories of Belgium in 1951 and Sweden in 1955 and 1958. A competition for 250cc machines, named the Trophée des Nations, was created by the FIM in 1961. Victories in the first years were clinched by the Swedes after two initial successes for Great Britain. In the 500cc team event Great Britain lost against Sweden in 1961/62, but then won five years in a row, reaching the number of 14 victories in 20 years, however their last win was in 1967 and it would take them another 27 years before winning again.

## **HARD FIGHTS**

**In Kishinev – then in the Soviet Union – the Russian team took the Chamberlain Trophy for the first time. Then it was time for Belgium, as they won again at Farleigh Castle in 1969, after chasing the trophy for 18 years. Legendary names of Roger de Coster, Joël Robert and Sylvian Geboers took their team to victory.**

The 70s saw a big change on a mechanical level, with the massive arrival of Japanese manufacturers to Europe. Suzuki started first, quickly followed by Yamaha, Honda and Kawasaki. The first years of the decade were shared between Sweden and Belgium.

In 1974 team USA made their debut at the event, with Jim Pomeroy (Bultaco), Brad Lackey (Husqvarna), Marty Tripes (Husqvarna) and Jimmy Weinert (Kawasaki) racing for the United States. That year they finished second, just ahead of the Russian squad including the 250cc World Champion Guennady Moisseev.

In 1975 in Czechoslovakia, the local team finished ahead of Belgium – despite the presence of three World Champions, Roger de Coster, Harry Everts and Gaston Rahier – and Great Britain. Two wins of the Belgian team preceded the second victory of the Russian team, still with Guennady Moisseev. In the Trophée des Nations contest, Belgium conquered all titles except one (Russia in 1979) as of 1969 until 1980.

In the Motocross des Nations Belgium won two more titles in ’79 and ’80, before the American dominance came into effect. Having missed both events in the two previous years, the American team arrived at Lommel in Belgium in September 1981 for the Trophée des Nations with four Honda riders managed by the former World Champion Roger de Coster. Danny Laporte, Chuck Sun, Johnny O’Mara and Donnie Hansen as they destroyed the opposition, and one week later won again in the Motocross des Nations in Bielstein, Germany.

Changes were made in the team from one year to the next, but it did not change anything. The American field was full of top riders during the 80s, and De Coster’s team got eight victories (four in each team competition) in four years. In 1980 the FIM decided to introduce a third Motocross team contest with 125cc motorcycles: the Coupe des Nations. The Italian team led by Michele Rinaldi won the first two titles, followed by Belgium (with Eric Geboers) and the Netherlands (with Kees Van der Ven and John Van de Berk).

**157**

## Pagina 158

MXoN HISTORY **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

# **NEW FORMAT**

**In 1985, a new format combining all three classes in a single Motocross des Nations event was introduced by the FIM, gathering all three classes (125cc, 250cc and 500cc) with one rider in each class and three races per event: 125/500, 125/250, 250/500.**

The Americans could not be stopped for over a decade, apart from 1994, as they went on to claim 17 consecutive victories (13 in the Motocross des Nations, and the last four of the Trophée des Nations). Their teams always integrated world-recognized top riders, with Danny “Magoo” Chandler winning all four heats in the team contests in 1982, but perhaps the greatest win of all came at Maggiora in 1986 when America’s trio of David Bailey, Ricky Johnson and Johnny O’Mara went through all three heats unbeaten by the rest of the world.

After Great Britain’s win in 1994, it was Belgium’s turn to be back on top of the competition in 1995 with Stefan Everts, Joel Smets and Marnicq Bervoets. But the Americans were still there. In ‘96 in Jerez, the team driven by multi-champion Jeremy McGrath beat the French and Belgian teams. Then for the following three years, Team USA missed out on a podium appearance, which allowed Belgium to claim two more victories as well as a first crown for Italy, with World Champion, Andrea Bartolini and Alessio Chiodi and Claudio Federici flying the flag in Brazil, in 1999, ahead of France and Belgium.

Just a year later the Americans were back at St Jean d’Angély, led by their new top rider Ricky Carmichael. In Namur 2001, it was finally the turn of the French team to clinch their first victory with David Vuillemin, Yves Demaria and Luigi Séguy, beating the Belgians on their home track. Italy won a perturbed 2002 edition, and Belgium – still with the record holder in individual World titles Stefan Everts – won the 2003 and 2004 contest, before the US comeback in the last five editions, as James Stewart replaced Carmichael as the team’s top rider in 2006. Carmichael was back in 2007, celebrating his last Motocross appearance in Budds Creek while Stewart was part of the 2008 winning team.

In 2009 the event came back to Italy after an absence of 23 years, this time in Franciacorta. The American Team won the Chamberlain Trophy again but failed in repeating their countrymen’s performance in 1986 at Maggiora. The trio made of Ryan Dungey, Jake Weimer and Ivan Tedesco struggled against, France, Belgium and Italy. Eventually USA won from France and Belgium while a crash of Italy’s top rider and multi-time World Champion Antonio Cairoli in heat three ended the team’s hopes of victory.

In 2010 the FIM Motocross of Nations was back in the USA where the home riders obtained their 21<sup>st</sup> Chamberlain Trophy in Lakewood, Denver, thanks to the performance of Ryan Dungey, Trey Canard and Andrew Short. The German team, who was leading the standings after race two thanks to the superb racing of youngster Ken Roczen and Max Nagl, were finally second followed by the Belgian team in third.

The following year it was a turning point for the event, as Monster Energy played an important role and became the title sponsor of the 2011 FIM Motocross of Nations. Saint Jean d’Angely in France was the chosen circuit for the 65<sup>th</sup> edition of the MXoN and the French fans witnessed a dramatic ending. The French team got into the final heat leading the classification with a nine points advantage over team USA, but in the decisive heat Pourcel ended up with an unfortunate DNF allowing the Americans to obtain their 22<sup>nd</sup> victory ahead of France and Australia, who were very consistent throughout the whole event.

In 2012, the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations returned to Lommel, the same circuit where Team USA started to dominate the event beating the Belgians on home soil in 1981. During the 2012 edition Team USA struggled a lot in the deep sand of the famous Belgian circuit and could only manage to finish on the third step of the podium, breaking their impressive seven-win streak. German team was the most consistent and obtained their maiden Chamberlain Trophy, while the home team finished with a well-deserved second position.

2013’s Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations in Teutschenthal, Germany, was probably one of the most anticipated events ever in the history of motocross. Heading into the event Team Germany would be defending the crown on their home turf while Team Belgium and USA would be lining up with hopes of redeeming themselves after a bitter defeat the previous year in Lommel, Belgium. While the pressure was laid heavily upon the local boys Team Germany’s Max Nagl, Ken Roczen and Dennis Ullrich, a crash which resulted in injury for Dennis Ullrich soon blew the teams hopes of a podium finish leaving the race to be ruled by Team Belgium and Team USA. With the final waving of the chequered flag it was surprisingly Team Belgium who narrowly took the victory ahead of Team USA. While the biggest surprise was Italy’s third place finish after their captain, seven-time FIM Motocross World Champion, Antonio Cairoli dominated both his races for the second year in a row.

Onto 2014, the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations tackled the loamy circuit of Kegums, Latvia for the first time in history of the track. With the base of the circuit being classed as sandy, yet nothing like Lommel, the defending champions Team Belgium were the favourites. Of course, Team USA had their devoted fans backing them too while the eventual winners Team France were the most criticised team going in with all three riders, Gautier Paulin, Steven Frossard and Dylan Ferrandis being Kawasaki riders and KTM’s FIM MX2 World Champion Jordi Tixier being excluded from the 2014 squad. Despite the harsh criticism, Team France couldn’t be flawed on the weekend as Paulin rode amazing to dominate every race, which was earnestly backed up by the outstanding performances of teammates Frossard and Ferrandis. With Team France being victorious, Team Belgium also rode solid with their team of Jeremy Van Horebeek, Kevin Strijbos and Julien Lieber for second while Team USA made up of Ryan Dungey, Eli Tomac and Jeremy Martin were dropped back to the third step of the podium.

## Pagina 160

MXoN HISTORY **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

In 2015 history was made as Team France, Romain Febvre, Gautier Paulin and Marvin Musquin, lined up as the defending champions on home soil and won! It was the first time Team France have ever won back-to-back and only their third time ever winning the event which is known as the summit of the season. What made the 2015 edition of the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations extra special was the fact it was held in Ernée, an entire decade after the memorable 2005 Motocross of Nations which took place on the very same track. Team France were by far the hot favourites heading in with Romain Febvre being the reigning World Champion and Gautier Paulin the runner up in the prestigious MXGP series. Marvin Musquin added to the already lethal French team as the runner up in the American motocross championship.

The ones expected to threaten France’s throne were, as always, the Americans. Representing the stars and stripes in 2015 was, Justin Barcia, Jeremy Martin and Cooper Webb flew the flag high. All three riders put in solid performances with Barcia winning in MXGP while Martin and Webb backed him up with silver medals. It went down to the wire, but unfortunately no-one could match the speed of MXGP’s gold plate holder, riding for France, Romain Febvre and also that of Marvin Musquin, both Frenchmen won their respective classes, Open and MX2, while Gautier Paulin also put in a solid ride for 4<sup>th</sup> in MXGP.

With the final waving of the chequered flag, Team France won by a narrow 2 points over Team USA, while Team Belgium, Jeremy Van Horebeek, Julien Lieber and Ken De Dycker, managed to round off the podium. The Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations reached another level of epicness in 2015. The venue was packed with an awesome crowd unafraid of showing their enthusiasm and love for motocross.

In 2016 we expected nothing less as the event took place in Maggiora, Italy, two whole decades since it played host to one of the greatest events in history, the 1986 Motocross of Nations. Italy provided a fabulous crowd, as it is known as one of the most motocross mad countries in the world. The 2016 Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations brought the American team of Jason Anderson, Cooper Webb, and Alex Martin to compete against team France.

Team France, came into the weekend with a goal of winning the Chamberlain Trophy for third consecutive year, was represented by Romain Febvre, Gautier Paulin, and Benoit Paturel. In the first race Glenn Coldenhoff of the Netherlands grabbed the holeshot but shuffled back after being passed by Italy’s Antonio Cairoli. It was Romain Febvre making moves though, he charged forward passing several riders including Cairoli taking the race win for France, Strijbos, Webb, and Van Horebeek rounded out the top 5. Race 2 was all team USA’s Jason Anderson out front. Battles ensued behind him between Herlings, Paulin, and Tonus. Herlings finished in second after remounting his KTM due to a slide out, Paulin then made his way to the podium ahead of Tonus.

Team USA was then in the points lead but immediately suffered a major blow when Anderson was landed on after crossing the finish. The third and final race of the day had Herlings in the lead, and it was left to Cooper Webb for the United States; Webb was positioned well in the race but eventually fell and lost ground. The deciding moment wasn’t until the final lap where France’s Romain Febvre made his way around the Kawasaki of Searle. Herlings won the race but the late move from Febvre gave France the single point they needed to win in epic form, the Netherlands finished second followed by team USA.

In 2017 the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations returned to the historic and epic venue of Matterley Basin, United Kingdom for the first time in 11 years. Among the top challengers for the Chamberlain Trophy would again be the winner of the last 3 consecutive Motocross of Nations, Team France who was made-up of Gautier Paulin, Romain Febvre, and Christophe Charlier. Looking to the steal the title as the best country in the world of motocross was the Netherlands Jeffrey Herlings, Glenn Coldenhoff and Brian Bogers and the UK’s Max Anstie, Dean Wilson, and Tommy Searle.

As the gate dropped for race one for team Netherlands, Glenn Coldenhoff took the Fox holeshot but the hometown hero, Max Anstie was right there and took the lead for Great Britain. Behind Coldenhoff was France’s Gautier Paulin who took over second when Coldenhoff made mistake over a jump. At the end of race 1 Anstie won ahead of a hard charging Tim Gajser and Gautier Paulin. Prior to race 2 staring France was already in the lead of the nation’s classification ahead of the Netherlands as team Great Britain’s Tommy Searle had bike issues in race 1. Race 2 was the Open and MX2 categories and the Netherlands and France were again strong with Herlings leading and Febvre in second. Herlings crashed on the same jump which his teammate, Coldenhoff, made a mistake but the “Bullet” shot back to the front of the field with a pass on Febvre. Herlings won the race ahead of Febvre and the USA’s Zach Osborne.

The 3<sup>rd</sup> and final race would decide it all and the 3-time defending champs of France still led the way over the Netherlands. Anstie took the final holeshot but Febvre was right with him. Anstie later made a mistake on the same jump as Coldenhoff and Herlings did prior handing the lead over to Febvre. While Anstie rebounded and fought back to the lead Herlings did his best passing Febvre as well but it would be too little too late as Anstie won ahead of Herlings and Febvre.

When the scores were tallied it was clear that France was the victor with 10 point less than the Netherlands who would again have to settle for 2<sup>nd</sup> while the home team, Great Britain, returned to the podium for the first time in 20 years!

## Pagina 161

MXoN HISTORY **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

2018 saw the return of the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations to USA at none other than the famous RedBud circuit in Buchanan, Michigan. The American venue was packed with one of the largest MXoN crowds ever seen who gloriously showed their enthusiasm and love for global motocross racing. The home team USA was led by the country’s best riders Eli Tomac, Justin Barcia, and Aaron Plessinger and cheered on by the patriotic and devoted American fans. Italy’s team of Antonio Cairoli, Alessandro Lupino, and Michele Cervellin put together an incredible weekend finishing 2<sup>nd</sup> only 2 points back of 1<sup>st</sup> .

Meanwhile the team from the Netherlands took the final spot on the podium as Jeffrey Herlings and Glenn Coldenhoff showed incredible heart with 1-2-1-1 finishes after the Dutch MX2 rider Calvin Vlaanderen was out with an injury. It was team France’s Gautier Paulin, Jordi Tixier, and Dylan Ferrandis making history again however, lined up as the defending champions they continued the French domination of the Chamberlain Trophy for the 5<sup>th</sup> year in succession!

In 2019, the 73<sup>rd</sup> edition of the Motocross of Nations headed to the Netherlands, hosted at the famous Assen TT circuit. This was the much-anticipated year for the Dutch fans, as Team Netherlands were the heavy favourites going into the weekend, having finished on the podium for the past few years.

The team, led by Glenn Coldenhoff, who went 1-1the previous year at RedBud, USA, alongside Jeffrey Herlings and Calvin Vlaanderen, dominated the rest of the world, making history as they won for the very first time and they did so in front of their home crowd. Coldenhoff repeated his 1-1 result as he won in the Open class, while Herlings and Vlaanderen finished 2-4, 10-10. Joining the team for this special occasion was not only thousands of Dutch fans, but also the King of the Netherlands. His Majesty King of The Netherlands Willem-Alexander took the opportunity to meet with the riders to mark the historic occasion.

Team Belgium’s Jeremy Van Horebeek, Jago Geerts and Kevin Strijbos had a strong ride and finished in 2<sup>nd</sup> with 47 points, while Team Great Britain made their third podium appearance in a row, as Shaun Simpson, Nathan Watson and Adam Sterry stood proudly on the final step of the podium 11 points behind the Belgian Team.

# **COVID-19 AND RETURN OF MXoN**

**With the world being struck by the COVID-19 outbreak in 2020, resulting in many travel restrictions and the FIM Motocross World Championship calendar heavily affected, Infront Moto Racing were forced to cancel what would have been the 74**<sup>**th**</sup> **edition of the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations in Ernée, France. It was an extremely tough call, but travel and quarantine rules would have made it hard for many of the national teams to compete.**

But with such a demanding year in the books, things were looking up for 2021, as the Monster Energy FIM MXoN headed to the home of motorsport, with the event taking place in Mantova, Italy!

The re-scheduled 74<sup>th</sup> edition delivered outstanding action and in true MXoN style, the final race was a nail-biter! Three nations, Italy, Great Britain and 2019 winners the Netherlands were among the favourites in the final laps of the race and with a pending penalty for Team Italy’s Alessandro Lupino it almost looked like the Italian team were out of the run, but what Antonio Cairoli, Alessandro Lupino and Mattia Guadagnini were able to deliver throughout the twoday event left no questions that they were deserving winners by the final chequered flag!

Team Italy was victorious for the first time in 19 years with 37 points, which was also the first MXoN victory for Antonio Cairoli as he announced his retirement at the end of 2021 – making for an even more special moment!

Team Netherlands placed second by just a single point, followed by Team Great Britain’s Shaun Simpson, Ben Watson and Conrad Mewse who were third a further point behind.

# **USA BACK ON TOP!**

**In 2022, the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations returned to one of the most legendary tracks in America, that is RedBud. Host of the 2018 event, this time around the organisers were keen on delivering an even bigger and better event than we’ve seen before and it’s fair to say that they managed to pull it off – even with the rain (again)!**

A total of 34 teams, including the newly introduced FIM Europe and FIM Latin America teams lined-up for the event. The FIM Europe and FIM Latin America squads gave opportunity for riders whose nations did not participate in the event, the chance to race and get the experience.

The qualifying day saw Team USA start their journey for redemption, as the squad topped the charts with three points ahead of France and Australia, thanks to Justin Cooper’s victory in the MX2 heat while Belgian Jago Geerts was victorious in the MXGP class, as Frenchman Dylan Ferrandis won the Open qualifying.

As the rain poured on Sunday, this did not stop the fun as the fight for the top step, as always, went right down to the wire. Coming into the final race USA led the way with France and Australia not too far behind, and while France looked like they had a chance to close in on the Americans, the crash from Dylan Ferrandis out of the lead cost the squad some points, but also Eli Tomac’s charge through the field kept USA in control.

It was a dream end at the 75<sup>th</sup> edition of the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations with home heroes, Team USA, finally putting an end to their 11-year winless streak as Eli Tomac, Justin Cooper and Chase Sexton delivered some very solid results to put the American flag back on top for the first time since 2011!

## Pagina 162

MXoN HISTORY **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

Team USA finished their weekend on 16 points, while Maxime Renaux, Marvin Musquin and Dylan Ferrandis for Team France ended up with 23 points ahead of Team Australia’s Mitch Evans, Hunter Lawrence and Jett Lawrence who had 26 points.

# **7**<sup>**TH**</sup> **HEAVEN FOR TEAM FRANCE**

**The 2023 Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations in Ernée, France, witnessed history as it boasted the largest crowd ever seen. With an astonishing 102,000 attendees over the weekend, the event's atmosphere was nothing short of unique and incredible. This extraordinary off-track enthusiasm fuelled the riders to deliver a jaw-dropping performance on the track, making it a truly memorable experience for all motocross fans.**

On the qualifying day, Team France embarked on their quest to reclaim the Chamberlain Trophy, as the squad topped the standings with a perfect two points ahead of Spain and Australia. This outstanding act was achieved through Tom Vialle's triumph in the MX2 RAM Qualifying Heat and Maxime Renaux's victory in the Open RAM Qualifying Heat. In the MXGP class, the 2023 MXGP World Champion, Jorge Prado, made a powerful statement to his competitors by clinching victory in the MXGP RAM Qualifying Heat.

On a beautiful and sunny Sunday, the atmosphere reached even greater heights. The first race saw an intense battle reminiscent of the MXGP season, with Romain Febvre and Jorge Prado pushing each other to the limits. The crowd erupted with excitement as Febvre made the final pass on Prado to win the race, propelling Team France to the top of the leader board, with Tom Vialle finishing 8<sup>th</sup> .

Race 2 was once again a dominant display by Team France, led by Maxime Renaux, who overtook Ruben Fernandez to take the lead, while Tom Vialle secured an impressive 2<sup>nd</sup> place, resulting in an extraordinary 1-2 finish for the French team. Race 3 was a formality for Team France, as they maintained their focus, while fierce battles unfolded for the 2<sup>nd</sup> and 3<sup>rd</sup> podium spots. Jett Lawrence and Ken Roczen engaged in thrilling battles, ultimately leading to the Australian victory and Team Australia's best-ever 2<sup>nd</sup> -place finish, with Hunter Lawrence and Dean Ferris. Team Italy secured the 3<sup>rd</sup> spot due to their consistency, led by Alberto Forato, Andrea Adamo, and Andrea Bonacorsi, finishing in front of a strong German team.

Team France, led by Romain Febvre, Tom Vialle, and Maxime Renaux, secured a resounding victory on their home turf with 14 points, much to the delight of the French crowd. Team Australia, represented by Jett and Hunter Lawrence and Dean Ferris, claimed the 2<sup>nd</sup> position with 34 points. Team Italy, with Alberto Forato, Andrea Adamo, and Andrea Bonacorsi, clinched the 3<sup>rd</sup> spot with 43 points.

This 76<sup>th</sup> edition of the Motocross of Nations will be remembered as an extraordinary event, where Team France's exceptional performance saw them clinch the Chamberlain Trophy for the 7<sup>th</sup> time in their history, making it a truly memorable occasion.

# **THUNDER FROM DOWN UNDER**

**The 77**<sup>**th**</sup> **edition of the Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations saw some of the best racing in the long history of the event, as World Champions Jorge Prado, Tim Gajser, Jeffrey Herlings, Romain Febvre, Tom Vialle, and Kay de Wolf engaged in combat with American legends Eli Tomac and Cooper Webb, German superstar Ken Roczen, and the highly talented Australian brothers Jett and Hunter Lawrence. Back at the massively popular Matterley Basin circuit in the UK, the weekend saw several pitched battles between the multiple Champions that will live long in the memory!**

After qualifying day victories for newly crowned double MXGP Champion Prado, the returning French MX2 ace Vialle, and the global force of Jett Lawrence, Spain would get first gate pick in Sunday’s races ahead of France and Australia. Team GB, led by 2017 MXGP winner Max Anstie in MX2 and Conrad Mewse in MX Open, raised their home fans’ hopes with fourth position ahead of Germany and The Netherlands, while Team USA had a difficult Saturday, finishing it in 9<sup>th</sup> position.

The opening race on Sunday saw the familiar sight of Prado and Gajser battling at the front, with Herlings giving chase just ahead of Tomac, Roczen, and Febvre! As the Slovenian passed Prado on the opening lap and began to pull away, Herlings dropped the bike right in front of Febvre!

Lucas Coenen was a revelation on the MX2 bike as he attacked Roczen for fifth, but then crashed and broke his collarbone! Gajser powered to an impressive race win as Tomac and Febvre both got past Prado to take second and third, with Herlings fifth ahead of his teammate, reigning MX2 World Champion De Wolf. Team Netherlands therefore led after race one ahead of the USA. In third were Australia, who had seen Hunter Lawrence recover from a bad start to grab eighth, while their MX2 rider Kyle Webster took second in his class with a fine eleventh place.

The second race saw Ruben Fernandez take the Fox Holeshot Award ahead of the MX2 machines of Simon Laengenfelder and Latvia’s B-Final winner Karlis Reisulis, who would take the Ricky Carmichael Award for the best young racer with second overall in MX2. Jett Lawrence got moving quickly though, passing his fellow HRC rider by the end of lap one to take a lead that he would never relinquish. De Wolf fought the 450s hard, passing the American Aaron Plessinger several times through the race due to mistakes. He would ultimately claim fifth, sealing the overall MX2 victory. Team Netherlands still held the lead despite P18 for Glenn Coldenhoff, but Team USA were just a point behind, with Australia in third heading into the final race!

That race was a spectacle that few will ever forget. Eli Tomac has never been a holeshot artist, but the American captain blasted into the lead from the start, then swapped places with Prado in one of several back-and-forth battles that made this race special. Gajser soon whipped past Prado for second on lap four.

**162**

## Pagina 163

MXoN HISTORY **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

The Slovenian then slugged it out with Tomac to take a clear lead by lap nine, but reigning Champions France saw their chances disappear with the exit of the injured Febvre. Jett Lawrence started to pick up the pace, and skipped past Tomac for second, then closed in on Gajser. The battle at the front for the last five laps was spell-binding, as the two HRC men both pushed to the maximum! Jett pulled an incredible pass on the five-time World Champion to make what looked to be the winning move!

Clearly, Gajser had not been given the script. He dug in, and on the final uphill triple jump, Tim changed his flight path and dived to the inside of the penultimate corner, requiring Jett to chop the throttle mid-turn! The crowd truly went wild, and the MXGP Vice-Champion kept the inside line through the final corner to win by under half a second!

With Tomac third, the USA beat the Netherlands to second overall. Hunter took fourth in race three, and Australia were finally crowned Champions after going from third to second to first in the last three competitions!

Kyle Webster did his bit behind the fastest brothers on the planet, and the Australians deserve their title, but the joy of this event was the wide-open racing all weekend! Matterley Basin was very special, and the flame of the Motocross of Nations continues to burn ever brighter.

# **THE AUSSIES STRIKE TWICE**

**The 78**<sup>**th**</sup> **Monster Energy FIM Motocross of Nations brought another memorable weekend to Ironman Raceway in Crawfordsville, Indiana. Under sunny skies and in front of more than 76,000 fans, some of the sport’s biggest names lined up once again, world champions Romain Febvre, Kay de Wolf and Tim Gajser, last year’s winning Australian trio of the Lawrence brothers and Kyle Webster, and home heroes Eli Tomac, Justin Cooper and RJ Hampshire.**

Race Two shifted the spotlight to Hunter Lawrence. A small early mistake briefly handed the lead to Fabio Santos and Jan Pancar, but eventually Hunter passed them and pulled away. Shimoda and Pancar followed with strong rides, while RJ Hampshire endured two heavy crashes that disrupted Team USA’s momentum. Webster had to pick up his bike from the ground as well but managed to finish 13<sup>th</sup> , keeping Team Australia on the first place in front of Team USA and Team France.

The last race delivered the kind of twists the MXoN is known for. Shimoda grabbed the holeshot, Coenen followed and both Lawrence brothers moved forward quickly. That changed when Jett collided with Isak Gifting at the top of Godzilla Hill, sending both riders to the ground. Hunter took advantage and passed Shimoda to take the lead, while Tomac charged into second to keep American hopes alive. Behind them, Febvre and Renaux worked to defend France’s overall position.

Jett began a determined comeback ride, slicing through the field while the Nations standings tightened lap by lap. Gajser pushed into the top three, Belgium and Slovenia gained ground and then came the turning point: Isak Gifting passed Maxime Renaux late in the race, a move that would drop France from second to third overall.

Up front, Hunter Lawrence held steady for the win, Tomac secured second, and Jett completed a remarkable recovery to take third. With those results, Australia claimed their second-straight Chamberlain Trophy, while the USA narrowly edged out France for the runner-up spot.

Ironman Raceway once again delivered a fantastic MXoN atmosphere, and as the weekend wrapped up, the attention already began shifting toward Ernée 2026.

Qualifying Day already showed how close the racing would be. Jett Lawrence set the fastest MXGP time, the new world champion Febvre topped his group, and Kay de Wolf was the quickest in MX2. Team USA looked solid with good speed from Cooper and Tomac. All the favourites appeared to be wellprepared for Sunday’s races.

Race One opened with Lucas Coenen snatching the Fox Holeshot, but Jett Lawrence wasted no time taking control, setting the tone for upcoming day. Early crashes for Ken Roczen and Romain Febvre reshuffled expectations, while Tomac pushed forward and pressured Gajser for third. De Wolf again showed why he is MX2’s standout by finishing as the top 250cc rider. Kyle Webster made one of the key rides of the race, recovering from a first turn crash to reach 15<sup>th</sup> . After the opening race, Australia, USA and France were separated by only a few points.

Only 6D Helmets have patented Omni-Directional Suspension®, an active in-helmet technology system that dramatically reduces energy transfer to the brain from low, mid, and high-velocity impacts for both linear and angular accelerations. Learn more at 6Dhelmets.com. SUSPENSION FOR YOUR HEAD.

## Pagina 179

MXoN REGULATIONS **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

# 2026 FIM MOTOCROSS OF NATIONS REGULATIONS

## **PROGRAMME FOR THE 2026 MONSTER ENERGY FIM MOTOCROSS OF NATIONS**

**MXGP (MORE THAN 40 RIDERS)**

**DURATION PER SESSION MAXIMUM NUMBER OF RIDERS**

|**DAY 1**|||
|---|---|---|
|1 X Free Practice MXGP|40 minutes|X riders|
|1 X Free Practice MX2|40 minutes|X riders|
|1 X Free Practice “Open”|40 minutes|X riders|
|1 X QualifyingRace MXGP|20 minutes + 2 laps|X riders|
|1 X QualifyingRace MX2|20 minutes + 2 laps|X riders|
|1 X QualifyingRace “Open”|20 minutes + 2 laps|X riders|
|1 X C-Final(*)|20 minutes + 2 laps|39 riders|
|**DAY 2**|||
|1 X Warm-UpB-Final|15 minutes|39 riders|
|1 X Warm-UpMXN Group1|15 minutes|30 riders|
|1 X Warm-UpMXN Group2|15 minutes|30 riders|
|1 X B-Final|20 minutes + 2 laps|39 riders|
|1 X Race,MXN(MXGP + MX2)|30 minutes + 2 laps|40 riders|
|1 X Race,MXN(MX2 + “Open”)|30 minutes + 2 laps|40 riders|
|1 X Race,MXN(MXGP + “Open”)|30 minutes + 2 laps|40 riders|

(*) Will be run only when 37 or more teams are present

### **Each team consists of 3 riders:**

- One rider on a MXGP class motorcycle

- One rider on a MX2 class motorcycle (the maximum age limit of 23 years in the MX2 class does not apply in the FIM Motocross of Nations)

- One rider on an “Open” class motorcycle, which wmeets the requirements of the MXGP or the MX2 class, at the team’s option

### **Results / Races:**

- The five best results of each team will be taken into consideration

- The team winning the FIM Motocross of Nations is the one which has gained the lowest total number of points after having added the five best results of its riders irrespective of class

- In case of a tie, the results will be established as follows:

### **Results / Qualifying / B-Final / C-Final:**

- The two best results of each team will be taken into consideration

- In case of a tie, the best result of the third rider will be taken into consideration

- First place = 1 point, second place = 2 points, third place and so on

- The best 19 Teams in the Qualifying Races qualify for the FIM Motocross of Nations

- The team winning the B-Final qualifies for the FIM Motocross of Nations

- The team winning the C-Final qualifies for the B-Final

- **A** The team whose riders have obtained the highest number of first places, second places, etc. amongst the five best results

- **B** The team whose riders have obtained the highest number of first places, second places, etc. in the last Race (FIM MXGP + “Open”)

- **C** The team whose riders have obtained the highest number of first places, second places, etc. in the second Race (FIM MX2 + “Open”)

- **D** The team whose riders have obtained the highest number of first places, second places, etc. in the first Race (FIM MXGP + FIM MX2)

- **E** If a tie still exists, the teams in question will be placed equal.

# 2025 WMX WORLD CHAMPION LOTTE VAN DRUNEN

## **2025 WMX SEASON**

In her short but massively successful career, Lotte van Drunen has risen to the status of World Champion long before her 18<sup>th</sup> birthday, and is part of a new wave of talent that is raising the status of Women’s Motocross. However, she has never been quite so savagely tested as in the aftermath of her first world title, and the immediate season as defending Champion. Fighting through that adversity to retain her title must make the 2025 Gold Medal all the more precious for her growing collection.

For anybody still wondering, Lotte is not from the same Van Drunen family as former GP rider Marcel, or his EMX-level son Jayson. Her first success in international competition was winning races in the 2018 EMX65 qualifiers against current EMX250 rider Bradley Meisters and finishing tenth in her first EMX65 finals. In 2021, she was the top qualifier from the EMX85 North-West group, taking third overall, ahead of current EMX250 rider Gyan Doensen, in the Sardegna finale.

After taking to EMX125 briefly as her male competitors got stronger and faster, Lotte turned 15 in August 2022 and immediately signed up to race the WMX finale in Türkiye. Two seventh places, still on a 125cc bike, were impressive enough to earn a Kawasaki ride for 2023, and she spent the winter adapting to the 250cc four-stroke machine.

Lotte instantly shot to the top with an outstanding first race win at her first WMX race on the four-stroke, in her favoured sandy terrain at Riola Sardo in Sardegna. From Holeshot to chequered flag, she controlled the first race with ease, before a mid-pack start in the second race forced her to fight through into third place, enough for second overall.

## Pagina 182

WMX CHAMPION **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

Battling with four-time Champion Courtney Duncan and fellow teenage tearaway Daniela Guillen, Lotte was consistently in the top three in her first full season and scored a glorious double race victory on home sand at Arnhem, her first overall Grand Prix win. Third in the series was just reward for her efforts, and she signed a multi-year contract with Yamaha to set herself up for 2024 and beyond.

That 2024 season saw her battle with Guillen through the first few rounds, but steadily ease away, even in Daniela’s second home GP in Spain. A thumping double victory for the self-styled “Queen of Sand” at home in the Netherlands left Lotte with a massive gap to defend in the final round, and she held fast to become a World Champion for the first time!

From there, enjoying some time in the USA as preparation for her first title defence, she suffered a painful crash at a test track in Georgia, breaking her right ankle in three places and putting an entirely different look on her winter programme. It took her seven weeks to be able to walk and cycle again, and over two months to get back on a dirt bike.

Two further months of dedicated training saw her ready to take on the opening round, mercifully for her at the MXGP of Sardegna, a venue where she has lost just one race across the three GPs she has contested in the deep sand. Another double win was obviously massively emotional, and with crashes for her expected chief rival Guillen, it gave Van Drunen a serious head start towards her second title. The Spaniard came away with 23 points less than the Dutch heroine, while six-time Champ Kiara Fontanesi sat 17 points off the pace. Both scores would become significant.

Still far from her best on hard pack, Lotte fought through from poor starts at Lugo in north-western Spain, while Fontanesi battled through a race two downpour to snatch a home GP win away from Guillen. With Van Drunen scoring a 5-2, the podium

was vital, but it was clear that the former Champ was to be a serious challenger for the 2025 crown. That was backed up at round three in Germany, which Kiara again won to level Courtney Duncan for the all-time WMX win record. The New Zealander was sadly out of 2025 with health issues, and hopefully we will see her return in 2026. Lotte’s second race win at Teutschenthal prevented the Italian from levelling the series, and the first half of the season ended with just a six-point margin between the teenager and the mother-of-two!

Lotte was expected to clean up at her home GP, but another below-par start left her with too much to do in race two, losing the overall GP to Guillen in her first ever WMX defeat in her native land! Fortunately for her, Fontanesi had an even worse start and lost 14 points across the weekend, leaving a 20-point gap between the pair, with Guillen just one further back!

**182**

## Pagina 183

WMX CHAMPION **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

In a fascinating mirror of the veteran-versus-teenager battle in MXGP, Fontanesi was determined to become the first mother to ever win a world Motocross title, and did all she could with a record-setting GP victory on the hardpack of Türkiye. She was unable to hold back Guillen in race one, however, but the pursuers both made points on Van Drunen, who finished off the podium, behind Norwegian flyer Martine Hughes, for the only time all season after more mediocre starts! A five-point penalty for both GASGAS riders due to yellow flag infringements took the sting out of the tale, and a 16-point buffer looked tough to overcome, despite a host of Australian talent lined up to race at the final round in Darwin!

Once more, Guillen and Fontanesi shared the top two positions in both races, and Australian contenders Charli Cannon and Taylah McCutcheon looked like possible key players, but Lotte made sure of the situation to take third in race one. This left her needing just seventh place in race two, and after cutting inside Fontanesi to claim a final race Holeshot, Van Drunen bravely battled with both of her main rivals before bringing it home safely in fourth, securing the title by just seven points over the Italian legend!

**IN THE HISTORY OF WMX, THE GREAT CHAMPIONS OF STEPHANIE LAIER, FONTANESI AND DUNCAN HAVE ALL TAKEN THREE TITLES IN SUCCESSIVE YEARS, AND TWO OF THOSE RIDERS ARE LIKELY TO TRY AND PREVENT THE LATEST CHAMPION FROM DOING THE SAME! GUILLEN IS ANOTHER YOUNG CONTENDER EAGER FOR GLORY, SO ALTHOUGH LOTTE VAN DRUNEN HAS AGE ON HER SIDE AS SHE REACHES FOR THE RECORDS IN HER CAREER TO COME, THERE WILL BE PLENTY OF COMPETITION FOR HER TO DEAL WITH. FOR NOW THOUGH, THE YOUNGEST DOUBLE WORLD CHAMPION IN THE HISTORY OF THE SPORT WILL AIM FOR A SOMEWHAT LESS PAINFUL WINTER BEFORE SHE TRIES TO BECOME THE FIRST TEENAGER EVER TO BECOME A TRIPLE WORLD MOTOCROSS CHAMPION!**

# 2025 EMX250 CHAMPION JANIS REISULIS

**IT IS SAID SOMETIMES, MAYBE BY THOSE WHO YEARN FOR THE CHARACTERS OF YESTERYEAR, THAT MODERN ATHLETES DON’T HAVE PERSONALITY. IT IS TOUGH ENOUGH FOR TOP SPORTSPEOPLE IN THE 21**<sup>**ST**</sup> **CENTURY TO WORK AS HARD AND BE AS DEDICATED AS THEY HAVE TO BE IN THE ULTRA-PROFESSIONAL SPORTS WORLD, THAT TO THEN TRY AND PROJECT A PERSONALITY IS OFTEN PUT DOWN AS A LOW PRIORITY. AFTER ALL, THE RACING AND RESULTS ARE WHAT COUNT.**

## **RIDER PROFILE**

|DATE OF BIRTH|**25/03/2008**|
|---|---|
|NATIONALITY|**LATVIAN**|
|NICKNAME|**THE KILLER**|
|TEAM|**VHR VRT YAMAHA OFFICIAL**
**EMX250 TEAM**|

In this respect, Janis Reisulis will hardly win many of those people over. With a deadpan expression, rarely smiling or openly happy, the kid they call “The Killer” will never truly show the joy of the sport to fans. However, this very persona is a characteristic all of its own, very true to his Baltic European roots, and helps add an edge to his on-track charisma that has always very much spoken for itself.

A winner in virtually every series he has been able to complete in the last five years, there is no doubt that the younger of the Reisulis brothers is a coming force in the Motocross world. His first EMX125 campaign in 2022 saw him finish the season in third, behind Cas Valk and his brother Karlis, but then he took the EMX125 title after a season-long battle with Mathis Valin in 2023. Despite a false start to his EMX250 career in 2024 due to a torn ACL, his comeback in 2025 has been nothing short of spectacular.

Janis had finished fourth in the opening EMX250 round at Madrid before his injury, and returning to Spain for just his second event in the class, it was like he had been there for years. In the deep mud at Cozar he overcame a mid-race slip-over to re-take the lead from Mads Fredsoe, and win by 11 seconds by the flag. The mud limited him to 12<sup>th</sup> in race two, but he was clearly on the map. From there, a double win at St Jean d’Angely already marked him out as the alpha male of the class, and he took the red plate after Trentino despite a DNF in race two!

A mechanical failure in Switzerland left him with a tiny points lead over two new challengers. Reigning EMX125 Champ Noel Zanocz and rising Spanish star Francisco Garcia were each just two points behind Janis, but again he showed his class with complete domination through the mud of Portugal and Spain with a complete four race sweep on the Iberian Peninsula!

**188**

## Pagina 189

EMX CHAMPIONS **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

Despite defeat to Zanocz at Ernée, an emotional perfect weekend at his home GP put him in a commanding position, and he maintained the lead from there, despite an incident in Finland where his Hungarian rival Zanocz put him off the track! The tasty rivalry between the two will be one to watch in years to come as they both progress to MX2, and the ebullient Garcia will also get amongst them!

Janis returned to winning ways at Arnhem to really put the Championship beyond doubt, and he sealed the deal with second behind Garcia on the Saturday race in Türkiye! There is no question of the Latvian’s talent, and in an age where racing brothers have often prospered together, he will join his sibling Karlis in MX2 for 2025 and beyond. Janis will have been interested in seeing Mathis Valin scoring well in MX2, as he will put himself at that level. His “Killer” persona and self-confident aura that seems to accept victory as the inevitable result will add yet another shade of colour to an already dazzling, talent-stacked MX2 category. Watching the progress of Janis Martins Reisulis into his new GP career should be absolutely fascinating.

Winning six Italian youth Championships on his way through the ranks, the boy from the motorsports mecca of Bologna first emerged on the European scene in 2022, with top ten finishes in the South-West 85cc Qualifying round in Slovenia. Coming in with 20<sup>th</sup> place at the Finale at Loket, while future 125cc rivals Gyan Doensen and Filippo Mantovani took second and third behind local flyer Vitezslav Marek, the young rider wearing #298 got himself 15-19 results, one of only 14 riders to get amongst the points scorers in both races. His main 2025 title rival, Mano Faure, came home in sixth overall with 5-9 scores.

EMX CHAMPIONS **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

There was a serious improvement for Alvisi during the 2023 season, scoring podium finishes in all three South-West Qualifiers he competed in, with four second places across the races in France, Slovenia, and Croatia, to put him second behind countryman Riccardo Pini in the region’s points standings. Third behind Pini and future Racestore teammate Aron Katona in the semi-final at his local circuit of Gazzane di Preseglie, Nicolo truly delivered at the 2023 Finale. A week after placing just eighth overall in his first World Junior Championships appearance, he fought through to second in the opening race at Loket from a start outside the top five, before moving into the lead on the opening lap of race two and holding his nerve on a slippery circuit to bring home the EX85 Championship on his Team Castellari GASGAS!

His first year on a 125cc machine saw him join Thomas Traversini’s Racestore KTM outfit, and a shaky start to the EMX125 series in Sardegna was followed by a big crash at the following round in Trentino, as he went over the handlebars off a big jump, cracking his elbow as well as breaking several ribs. Unrelated to the crash, he was found to have a medical problem with his lungs that extended his absence from racing to over six weeks. After a couple of rounds of finding his feet, he put himself into the top three for the first time in the opening races at both the hardpack of Uddevalla and the sand of Arnhem. Then it all came together with a brilliant overall win around the grasslands of Frauenfeld, going 5-1 to outscore his countryman Francesco Bellei and Austrian Max Ernecker. It lifted him to 11<sup>th</sup> in the final standings while his teammate Doensen took second, and lined him up for a solid year ahead in 2025.

The opening round in the deep mud of Cozar gave us no indications for the season to come, as race winners Mantovani and Jekabs Kubulins would not win again all year, and the measly eight points for Alvisi was by far his lowest haul of 2025. Just missing the podium as one of four Italians in the top six at round two in France was a better example of what

he could do, especially as he completed a Racestore 1-2 with Katona in the second outing. Second overall in the sand of Sardegna put him right into the Championship equation, although his Hungarian teammate was on a three-race winning streak and powering ahead in the points.

An understandably steady sixth overall at Trentino saw him drop down a little, but from there the young Italian really started to hit his stride. Winning his first race of the year on his return to Frauenfeld, a battle back from a first corner crash in race two put him back on the podium, in another 1-2 with Katona. The pair now only had Bellei between them at the top of the points standings, although the Hungarian looked to be in complete control. Saying that, the Italian came out on top in a brilliant bar-banging battle between the pair in the opening race at Agueda, in what turned out to be the driest half-race of the whole weekend!

EMX CHAMPIONS **2026 OFFICIAL GUIDE**

After also winning a fight with his main rival to be, Mano Faure, Alvisi took second overall despite a seventh in the mud of race two. However, with Katona beginning a run of bad results which would culminate in a knee injury that put him out of the season, Nicolo stepped up to deliver the first perfect weekend of his EMX career at Ernée, that suddenly gave him the red plate! Another race win the following weekend in Germany put him up by 30 points over Faure, but the French kid suddenly went on a rich run of form to put some serious pressure on the Italian. The pair went 1-2 in Latvia with Faure gaining just two points, then the World Junior Championships saw a rough weekend for Alvisi, albeit his best ever result at that level with seventh overall! Not an event that he’s likely to miss much in the future…

Faure continued his fine form at Matterley Basin with a double win, while Alvisi got away with a big crash over the uphill triple to take just 17-4 finishes, which saw the pair level on points with two rounds to go! The final two rounds were also to be in the deep sand of Finland and Flanders, but Alvisi was not phased by this at all and dominated the racing at the new KymiRing by an average of eight seconds per race! Faure was second in both outings, however, fighting hard with late passes in race two to get there, so the gap was a tiny six points with just the tortures of Lommel ahead of them!

Faure had good support in the sand with Yamaha Europe teammates Jarne Bervoets and Dani Heitink on great form, but Alvisi made life tough for himself by starting outside the top twenty, falling on lap one, and crashing again on lap three! He fought through to sixth, which amazingly he kept despite another tumble on the last lap! With Faure second to Bervoets, however, Alvisi was suddenly down by a point with just one race to go!

The final race saw an incredible fight as Faure took his turn to hit the deck early, while Alvisi himself didn’t start well. The pair got together mid-race and the Italian came out on top, and pressed forward to take an amazing second in the race while Faure fell twice. In hot conditions through the punishing sand, Nicolo Alvisi dug in brilliantly to take his second European title in fine style!

As always with EMX125 Champions, the future looks bright. While we have to go back ten years to see the last EMX title for a future World Champ. In Jorge Prado, many of those since are still current factory riders with much opportunity ahead of them. For sure the young man from Bologna is one to watch as he heads into EMX250 with two European titles in his pocket from the last three years!

# 2025 EMXOPEN CHAMPION CEDRIC GROBBEN

**BELGIAN RIDER CEDRIC GROBBEN ENJOYED THE WEEKEND OF HIS LIFE AT LOMMEL, WHERE HE CLINCHED THE 2025 EMXOPEN EUROPEAN TITLE IN FRONT OF HIS HOME CROWD. COMING INTO THE EVENT WITH NO MAJOR EXPECTATIONS, GROBBEN KEPT HIS FOCUS SIMPLE. RIDE SMOOTH, STAY CONSISTEN AND MAKE THE MOST OF THE OPPORTUNITY. THAT APPROACH PAID OFF IN THE BEST POSSIBLE WAY.**

In the opening race he quickly moved past early leader Tom Guyon to take control, crossing the finish line more than 13 seconds ahead of Guyon. In race two, Grobben rode smart rather than risky. Climbing steadily through the pack to finish third behind the Baltic duo Mairis Pumpurs and Gert Krestinov. That 1–3 score was enough to secure the overall victory and the European crown.

Racing in the familiar deep sand of Lommel, Cedric showed his comfort and experience on one of the toughest tracks in Europe. He called it “the best race win of my career” and it’s easy to see why. His pace was sharp, his lines were clean and he handled the heavy conditions with ease and precision.

What makes Grobben’s success stand out even more is his background. Unlike many riders on the EMX grid, Cedric works a regular job during the week. Balancing full-time work with training and racing is no small task and that commitment often means tough choices. One of those being skipping the Dutch Masters this season, because he doesn’t have enough time to train.

The EMXOpen class has long been known as a mix of ambitious privateers and experienced national level riders and Cedric Grobben’s 2025 campaign was the perfect example of how far passion and persistence can take you.
